We also introduce another type of information geometric renormalisation of inferential dynamics of quantum states, which describes situations where none of specific control (covariate) parameter is fixed, but the quantum model is subjected to the action of completely positive maps. This procedure is based on the use of
D_{\mathfrak{f}}
distances as well as associated
contraction coefficients
, introduced by Ruskai et al
[
72
,
64
,
293
,
227
]
. For an alternative (and essentially more developed) approach to renormalisation based on
D_{\mathfrak{f}}
on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
, see
[
30
,
31
,
32
]
(c.f.
[
79
]
for a pedagogical introduction).

1.5
Locally quantum multi-user information relativity

The discussion in this paper is aimed at the construction of the framework of the multi-user (intersubjective) information relativity with emergent spaces. While most of discussion is kept in the framework build upon the W
∗
-algebras, it is more a useful testing ground (and a verifying constraint for backwards compatibility), then a desired property of the framework. In other words, our intention is to get rid of the
Postulate 1
of Section
1.1
by a suitable reformulation of the
Postulate 5
and
6
. We propose to consider the following tentative structure:

1)

Consider arbitrary set
\mathcal{M}
of users.

2)

each user
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
is equipped with his/her own vector space
V(\phi)
containing his/her descripton of ‘states’ (‘configurations’, ‘preparations’, ‘inputs’) and a dual vector space
V^{\mathbf{d}}(\phi)
, containing his/her description of ‘effects’ (‘registrations’, ‘results’, ‘outputs’).

3)

User’s notion of inference (respectively, causality) is modelled by a choice of set of of endomorphisms of
V(\phi)
(respectively,
V^{\mathbf{d}}(\phi)
).

4)

In particular, inferences on
V(\phi)
can be provided by means of the Brègman functional
\tilde{D}_{\Psi,\phi}:V(\phi)\times V(\phi)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
, while the causality on
V^{\mathbf{d}}(\phi)
can be provided either by the Legendre–Fenchel conjugate of
\tilde{D}_{\Psi,\phi}
, or by a representation of some Lie algebra.

5)

More specifically, if one wants to do statistical inference, it is necessary to make clear how the behaviour of finite data sets is associated with the specific idealisations used in the theoretic framework, as represented by the ‘ideal’ theoretical states and effects. In order to assert such relation, it is necessary to admit some statistical tools that are representing the control over a ‘convergence to an ideal form of a data set’, for any given nonideal form of a real data set. Large number estimation and asymptotic estimation are two typical tools (on the geometric level, they correspond, respectively to relative entropy maximisation, and the local linearity). If a user introduces a discrimination function (information potential, absolute entropy)
\Psi_{\phi}:V(\phi)\rightarrow\,]-\infty,+\infty]
, then he/she is able to quantify the rates of convergence of sequences of data. The key property of the Brègman functional is that it allows for a generalised pythagorean theorem (
144
), which is a nonlinear generalisation of the fundamental property of euclidean and Hilbert spaces. Yet, it is doing it without necessity of assuming that
V(\phi)
is normed, or even metrisable. As a result, user’s local inferences on
V(\phi)
based on entropic projections
\mathfrak{P}^{\tilde{D}_{\Psi,\phi}}
allow to decompose the information distance to an ‘ideal inference’ (
\tilde{D}_{\Psi,\phi}(x,y)
) into a sum of a distance to an effective solution (satisfying given constraints and minimising the distance) and an uncertainty within the constrained space. (By the Legendre–Fenchel duality, completely parallel considerations are applicable to causality on
V^{\mathbf{d}}(\phi)
determined by the relative free energy
\tilde{D}_{\Psi^{\mathbf{L}},\phi}:V^{\mathbf{d}}(\phi)\times V^{\mathbf{d}}(\phi)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
.)

6)

The geometric structures on the set
\mathcal{M}
are introduced as quantitative means of relating (synchronising) inferences and causality of different users.

7)

Each user
\phi
provides his/her own mappings from
\mathcal{M}
into
V(\phi)
, given by bijective embeddings
\ell_{\phi}:U(\phi)\rightarrow V(\phi)
, where
U(\phi)\subseteq\mathcal{M}
is the subset of users in
\mathcal{M}
that a user
\phi
considers as representable in terms of his/her ‘configuration’ space
V(\phi)
.

8)

Using the embeddings
\ell_{\phi}
, each user can relate his/her individual inferences on
V(\phi)
with other users using (a part of) the same set
U
. In particular, the relative Brègman entropy
D_{\Psi}
on
\mathcal{M}
is induced by

\tilde{D}_{\Psi,\phi}(\ell_{\phi}(\omega_{1}),\ell_{\phi}(\omega_{2}))=D_{\Psi,U(\phi)\cap U(\psi)}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\tilde{D}_{\Psi,\psi}(\ell_{\psi}(\omega_{1}),\ell_{\psi}(\omega_{2}))\;\;\forall\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in U(\phi)\cap U(\psi).

(26)

This provides the means to relate the
large number
inferential dynamics of different users (such as in Sanov’s theorem).

8)

The local smooth manifold structure induced by
D_{\Psi}
on
\mathcal{M}
provides the means to relate
asymptotic
inferential dynamics of different users. In particular, the equivalence of local geodesic projection and local
D_{\Psi}
projections can be understood as a method of locally linear synchronisation of inferences of different users. In such case the curvature of the manifold
\mathcal{M}
measures impossibility of ideal synchronisation of inferences between different users. The relationships with the notions of causality, effective dynamics, and renormalisation were discussed in the previous Section. They are dependent only on the notion of Brègman distance on an arbitrary set
\mathcal{M}
, and Eguchi equations on an arbirary smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}
, hence they hold in general, without assuming that
\mathcal{M}
is a subset of
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
for some W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
.

9)

The reconstruction of the special case, when
\mathcal{M}
is equal to the set
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
of states over a globally defined W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
is an open problem. Our conjecture is that quantum mechanics can be characterised as a set
\mathcal{M}
equipped with an induced structure of the riemannian manifold
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g}^{D_{\Psi}})
and a Poisson manifold such that the set of extremal points of the convex hull of
\mathcal{M}
admits an induced metric and induced symplectic structure that determine a Kähler manifold satisfying the standard properties of the quantum mechanical Kähler manifolds.

In principle, the above scheme is applicable to a wide class of postquantum information theoretical settings, such as general probabilistic theories. In what follows, having in mind the possible applications in nonequilibrium quantum statistical mechanics, we will focus on its implementation in the context of W
∗
-algebras and the associated functional analytic spaces.

While (
313
) considers only the case of local
\nabla^{1/2}
/
D_{1/2}
-projections,
[
139
,
211
,
215
]
consider only the case of global
D_{0}
-projections. Yet, we think that the natural geometric objects for construction of local and nonlocal quantum kinematics and dynamics are arbitrary Banach Lie algebras (to describe causality) and arbitrary quantum Brègman distances (to describe inference), connected together via dual pairs of noncommutative Orlicz spaces. More specifically, following a discussion in Section
1.2.2
we think that the problems considered in this paper should be readdressed in a more general foundational framework, based on the following principles:

1)

The use of GNS Hilbert bundle should be replaced by a suitable bundle of noncommutative Orlicz spaces
L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})
playing the role of tangent spaces
\mathbf{T}_{\phi}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, understood as the spaces of local ‘configurations’ (e.g.
\phi(\theta)\mapsto\theta\mapsto\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}\right)
), with their Banach duals
L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})^{\star}
playing the role of cotangent spaces
\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\phi}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, understood as the spaces of local ‘effects’ (
f(\phi)\mapsto\mathbf{d}f\mapsto(\mathbf{d}f_{i})
). Somewhat similar ideas were considered earlier in
[
341
,
233
]
, but only in a global context, restricted to a single ‘tangent’ and ‘cotangent’ space.

2)

There should be provided a canonical construction of a quantum Brègman distance
D_{\Psi}
associated with a Banach dual pair
(L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N}),L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})^{\star})
, a function
\Psi:L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and a family of embeddings
\ell_{\phi}:\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}\rightarrow L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})
, such that the second order Gâteaux derivative of
D_{\Psi}
determines a map

\mathbf{g}_{\phi}^{D_{\Psi}}:L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})\times L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})^{\star}\rightarrow[0,\infty],

(27)

while the third order Gâteaux derivatives determine the connections
\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}
and
(\nabla^{D_{\Psi}})^{\dagger}
, with the respective parallel transports equal to the isometric transition operators

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}}_{\phi,\omega}

\displaystyle:\mathbf{T}_{\phi}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathbf{T}_{\omega}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),

(28)

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{(\nabla^{D_{\Psi}})^{\dagger}}_{\phi,\omega}

\displaystyle:\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\phi}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\omega}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),

(29)

and satisfying the generalised Norden–Sen duality

\mathbf{g}^{D_{\Psi}}_{\phi}(\mathbf{t}^{\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}}_{\phi,\omega}(x),\mathbf{t}^{(\nabla^{D_{\Psi}})^{\dagger}}_{\phi,\omega}(y))=\mathbf{g}^{D_{\Psi}}_{\omega}(x,y).

(30)

This is intended to implement the principle discussed in Section
2.4.3
: the local structure of an information manifold should implement the local equivalence of an entropic projection (user’s inference) and a geodesic free fall (an absence of geometric ‘gravity’).

3)

Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
and Banach Lie algebras
\mathcal{B}
should be represented on
L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})^{\star}
, the latter giving rise to a Banach Lie–Poisson manifold structure on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
determined by the action of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
on
L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})
.

4)

As a result, both
\mathbf{d}_{\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}}(\phi)
and
\mathbf{d}h_{\mathcal{B}}(\phi)
become the elements of the same operator space
L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})^{\star}
, so the local effective dynamics can be formulated in terms of a
1
-form
{\cal F}=\mathbf{d}h_{\mathcal{B}}+\mathbf{d}_{\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}}
, treating local causality and local inference on an equal footing. Note that, in face of the discussion in Sections
1.2.1
,
1.2.2
, and
2.4.3
, the condition
{\cal F}=0
can be understood as an information-theoretic analogue of Einstein’s version of Newton’s first law of motion: a rest in a local causal-inference frame (provided by user’s choice of generators
\mathcal{B}
for causal dynamics in a cotangent space of effects, and a discrimination function
\Psi
for inferential dynamics in a tangent space of states).

5)

The local tangent BLP hamiltonian vector field, generating a local W
∗
-dynamical system associated with a local fiber of a GNS Hilbert bundle, should be represented in terms of
L_{\Upsilon}
-liouvillean, acting on the
L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})
tangent space. The incorporation of the contribution of the local free fall along
\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}
-connection geodesics should be provided by the perturbation of this
L_{\Upsilon}
-liouvillean, defined in an analogy with the Jakšić–Pillet
L_{1/\gamma}
-liouvillean
[
165
]
.

6)

The local entropic prior should be constructed using
D
and
\mathbf{g}^{D}
representing the
effective geometry
of a model
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, after the renormalisation of all contributions from control sources. One can interpret the local entropic prior at a point
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
as an information theoretic analogue of the (inferential) mass of the user at
\phi
. This interpretation gives a particularly neat meaning to the observation
[
235
,
3
]
, discussed in Section
1.2
, that the regularising term in the Daubechies–Klauder formula leads to a point-dependence of a zero-point energy iff the curvature of the Fubini–Study metric is nonconstant. From our point of view, it means: the curvature
\kappa^{\nabla^{D}}
of a quantum model is a measure of desynchronisation of the ideal multi-user inference (as given locally by
\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}
-geodesics, or, equivalently,
\mathbf{d}_{\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}}
), reflected in the influence of a local mass on the local zero-point energy. The case of information model with a constant curvature is an inferential analogue of the Carathéodory–Jacobi–Lie theorem for symplectic manifold, allowing for a global trivial synchronisation of local causality systems.

7)

Given these constructions, the dynamical Ansatz (
313
) should be generalised by means of the replacement of the GNS Hilbert bundle by a corresponding dual bundle of noncommutative Orlicz spaces.

8)

In principle, every quantum Brègman functional
\tilde{D}_{\Psi}
on
L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})
determines the Legendre–Fenchel conjugate functional
\tilde{D}^{\mathbf{L}}_{\Psi}
on
L_{\Upsilon(\phi)}(\mathcal{N})
, which can be naturally interpreted as a relative free energy. If one would replace the use of the (nonlocally trivially synchronisable)
1
-form
\mathbf{d}h_{\mathcal{B}}
by the form
\mathbf{d}_{\nabla^{D^{\mathbf{L}}_{\Psi}}}
, then it would be necessary to introduce a corresponding local entropic prior (causal mass), measuring the influence of desynchronisation of local systems of causality, along the spatial trajectory on
\mathcal{M}
, on the effective quantitative nonlocal evolution. The postulate of the local cancellation of effects of those two (inferential and causal) priors, when integrated together, would be an information theoretic analogue of the postulate of equality of gravitational and inertial mass.

9)

In the JMF source theoretic approach the local departure of riemannian metric from the hessian geometry is described by the equation (
382
). Applying the change of geometry
\mathbf{g}^{D_{\Psi}}\mapsto\tilde{\mathbf{g}}
to a definition of a prior
\exp\left(-k\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\int_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathbf{g}_{ab}(\phi)\dot{\phi}^{a}\dot{\phi}^{b}\right)\sqrt{\det(\mathbf{g}(\phi))}
, we can see that the change of the scalar curvature
\kappa(\phi)
of the model is reflected in the redefinition of the local prior (information mass)
P
. This leads to a more general problem. Given any neighbourhood
U\subseteq\mathcal{M}
of a user
\phi\in\mathcal{M}
, one can ask how to determine the departure of a prior
P
on
U
(from the originally postulated one) that is caused by the changes of curvature
\kappa
of a model
\mathcal{M}
associated with a given system of inference, when the transformation of geometry of a model
\mathcal{M}
(e.g., due to the Jaynes–Mitchell renormalisation) is considered. The analogy with general relativity that we were pursuing in this paper, as well as an observation
[
365
]
that the uniform weighting of the trajectories requires constant scalar curvature, leads us to conjecture that

\delta\int_{U}P=\delta\int_{U}\kappa.

(31)

We hope that this mathematical framework will allow for a unified treatment of the foundations of nonequilibrium quantum statistical mechanics, as discussed from different perspectives in
[
120
]
,
[
341
]
, and
[
165
]
(see also
[
50
]
). More generally, we consider it to be a testing ground for a construction of a predictive dynamical theory that would unify several concepts of general relativity with quantum information theory into a single framework of multi-agent (post)quantum information relativity. Maybe this sounds a bit like a fountain of conjectures and high hopes, but we believe that the science fiction of today is just an advanced propagator of a scientific research of tomorrow.

2
Quantum geometry & global dynamics

Sections
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.4.1
,
2.4.2
do not contain new results or constructions, except of the definition of the quantum Poisson system.

2.1
Quantum Banach Lie–Poisson spaces

2.1.1
Banach–Lie–Poisson spaces

Let
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
. A vector space
X
over
\mathbb{K}
is called a
Lie algebra
iff it is equipped with a function
[\cdot,\cdot]:X\times X\rightarrow X
such that for all
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\in X
and for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}

1)

[f_{1},f_{2}]=-[f_{2},f_{1}]
(antisymmetry),

2)

[f_{1},f_{2}+\lambda f_{3}]=[f_{1},f_{2}]+\lambda[f_{1},f_{3}]
(linearity),

3)

[f_{1},[f_{2},f_{3}]]+[f_{3},[f_{1},f_{2}]]+[f_{2},[f_{3},f_{1}]]=0
(Jacobi identity).

The function
[\cdot,\cdot]
is called the
Lie bracket
. If
(X,[\cdot,\cdot])
satisfies also the Leibniz’s rule

4)

\{f_{1},f_{2}f_{3}\}=\{f_{1},f_{2}\}f_{3}+f_{2}\{f_{1},f_{3}\}\;\;\forall f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\in X
,

then
[\cdot,\cdot]
is called the
Lie--Poisson bracket

[
229
]
, while
(X,[\cdot,\cdot])
is called a
Lie--Poisson algebra
. If
G
is a Lie group, then a Lie algebra of its generators will be denoted
\mathrm{Lie}(G)
. A vector space
X
over
\mathbb{K}
is called a
Banach Lie algebra
iff it is a Banach space with a norm
{\left|\!\left|{\cdot}\right|\!\right|}
, a Lie algebra, and its Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]:X\times X\rightarrow X
is bilinear and continuous in the topology of
{\left|\!\left|{\cdot}\right|\!\right|}
. If
\mathfrak{g}
is a Banach Lie algebra, then the
adjoint map

\mathrm{ad}_{x}:\mathfrak{g}\ni y\mapsto[x,y]\in\mathfrak{g}
and
coadjoint map

\mathrm{ad}_{x}^{\star}:\mathfrak{g}^{\star}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\star}
,

\left[\!\left[y,\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{x}(z)\right]\!\right]_{\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\star}}:=\left[\!\left[\mathrm{ad}_{x}(y),z\right]\!\right]_{\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\star}}\;\;\forall z\in\mathfrak{g}^{\star},

(32)

are norm continuous for each
x\in\mathfrak{g}
.

Let
M
be a real Banach smooth manifold, and
{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R})
denotes the space of all infinitely Fréchet differentiable
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions on
M
. Then a real
Poisson structure
on
M
is defined as a function
\{\cdot,\cdot\}:{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R})\times{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R})\rightarrow{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R})
such that
({\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R}),\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is a Lie algebra
[
229
]
. If
M
above is
finite dimensional
, then
(M,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is called a real
Poisson manifold

[
228
,
367
,
354
,
368
]
. If
M
is a real Banach smooth manifold, then the cotangent space at
x
can be defined by
\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}M:=(\mathbf{T}_{x}M)^{\star}
, and each element of
\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}M
has a form

\mathbf{d}f(x)\equiv\mathbf{d}_{x}f:\mathbf{T}_{x}M\ni v\mapsto\mathbf{d}_{x}f(v):=v(f)\in\mathbb{R}

(33)

for some
f\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M:=\bigcup_{x\in M}\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M
, where
\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M:=(\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{x}M)^{\star}=(\mathbf{T}_{x}M)^{\star}{}^{\star}
. If
(M,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is a real Poisson manifold, then every
k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R})
determines a unique vector field
\mathfrak{X}_{k}\in\mathbf{T}M
by

\mathfrak{X}_{k}(f):=\bigcup_{x\in M}\left\{x\mapsto\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{X}_{k}(x),\mathbf{d}f(x)\right]\!\right]_{\mathbf{T}_{x}M\times\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}M}\right\}=\{f,k\}\;\forall f\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R}).

(34)

Such
\mathfrak{X}_{k}
is called a
hamiltonian vector field
, while the corresponding
k
is called the
Hamilton function
. If
M
is infinite dimensional, then the value of
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
at
x\in M
depends only on the differentials
\mathbf{d}f(x),\mathbf{d}k(x)\in\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}M
. Hence, there exists a section
\varpi
of the vector bundle
\bigwedge^{2}\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M
such that

\{f,k\}=\varpi(\mathbf{d}f,\mathbf{d}k).

(35)

Let
\varpi
be a
smooth section
, meaning that for each
x\in M
there exists a continuous bilinear antisymmetric function
\varpi_{x}:\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}M\times\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
x\mapsto\varpi_{x}
is smooth. Let the function
\natural:\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}M\rightarrow\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M
be the bundle map covering identity, isometric on fibres, and satisfying

\natural_{x}(\mathbf{d}f(x)):=\varpi_{x}(\cdot,\mathbf{d}f),

(36)

which means that

(\natural_{x}(\mathbf{d}f(x)))(\mathbf{d}k(x))=\{k,f\}(x)\;\;\forall f,k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R}).

(37)

Then

\mathfrak{X}_{k}:=\varpi(\cdot,\mathbf{d}k)=\natural(\mathbf{d}k)=\{\cdot,k\}

(38)

is a smooth section of
\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M
, but may not be a vector field on
M
, because
\mathbf{T}M\subseteq\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M
and
\mathbf{T}M\not\cong\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M
in general. In order to solve this problem, Odzijewicz and Ratiu
[
253
]
proposed (see also further discussion and results in
[
254
,
27
,
28
,
255
,
351
,
287
,
252
]
) to define a real
Banach Poisson manifold
as a pair
(M,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
of a real Banach smooth manifold
M
and a Poisson structure
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on it such that the function
\natural:\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}M\rightarrow\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}{}^{\maltese}M
defined above satisfies
\natural(\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}M)\subseteq\mathbf{T}M
.

If
(M,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is a real Banach Poisson manifold, then every
k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{R})
determines a unique vector field
\mathfrak{X}_{k}\in\mathbf{T}M
defined by (
38
), and called a
hamiltonian vector field
. Such
k
is then called the
Hamilton function
. Every real Poisson manifold is a real Banach Poisson manifold, so this terminology is consistent. Odzijewicz and Ratiu
[
253
]
define also a
holomorphic
Banach Poisson manifold which provides an analogous setting for
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
, but we will not use its specific properties here, so we omit its definition. If a Banach space
X
over
\mathbb{K}
is equipped with a Poisson structure
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
that turns it into a (real or holomorphic) Banach Poisson manifold, then
X^{\star}\subseteq{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(X;\mathbb{K})
. Moreover,
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is linear on
{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(X;\mathbb{K})
if
\{X^{\star},X^{\star}\}\subseteq X^{\star}
.

A
Banach--Lie--Poisson space
is defined
[
253
]
as a pair
(X,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
such that

1)

X
is a Banach space over
\mathbb{K}
,

2)

(X,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is a real (if
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
) or holomorphic (if
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
) Banach Poisson manifold,

3)

X^{\star}\subseteq{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(X;\mathbb{K})
is a Banach Lie algebra with respect to
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
.

In such case, the restriction of
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on
{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(X;\mathbb{K})
to
X^{\star}
will be denoted by
[\cdot,\cdot]
. As proved in
[
253
]
, the Banach space
X
is a BLP space
(X,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
iff
X^{\star}
is a Banach Lie algebra
(X^{\star},[\cdot,\cdot])
satisfying

\mathrm{ad}_{x}^{\star}(X)\subseteq X\subseteq X^{\star}{}^{\star}\;\;\forall x\in X^{\star},

(39)

and in such case
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is given by

\{f,k\}(z)=\left[\!\left[z,[\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{z}f,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{z}k]\right]\!\right]_{X\times X^{\star}}\;\;\forall f,k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(X;\mathbb{K})\;\forall z\in X.

(40)

Moreover, under those conditions the hamiltonian vector field associated to
any

k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(X;\mathbb{K})
reads

\mathfrak{X}_{k}(z)=-\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{z}k}(z)\;\;\forall z\in X.

(41)

If
(X,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is a BLP space, and if
X\cong\mathbf{T}_{x}X

\forall x\in X
, then
\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}X\cong(\mathbf{T}_{x}X)^{\star}\cong X^{\star}
, so if
f\in X^{\star}
, then one can identify
\mathbf{d}_{x}f\in\mathbf{T}_{x}^{\maltese}X
with
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{x}f\in X^{\star}
. As a result, for any
z\in X
and
y\in X^{\star}
the linearity of
y
gives
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{z}y=y
, and for every
x\in X
one has
[
38
,
253
]

\displaystyle\left[\!\left[y,\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{x}(z)\right]\!\right]_{X^{\star}\times X^{\star}{}^{\star}}

\displaystyle=\left[\!\left[z,[x,y]\right]\!\right]_{X\times X^{\star}}=\{x,y\}(z)=-\{y,x\}(z)

(42)

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{X}_{x}(y))(z)=\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{z}y,\mathfrak{X}_{x}(z)\right]\!\right]_{X^{\star}\times X^{\star}{}^{\star}}=-\left[\!\left[y,\mathfrak{X}_{x}(z)\right]\!\right]_{X^{\star}\times X^{\star}{}^{\star}}.

(43)

Hence,

\mathfrak{X}_{x}(z)=-\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{x}(z)\;\;\;\forall z\in X\;\forall x\in X^{\star}.

(44)

The notion of the BLP space can be viewed as a suitable generalisation of the important properties of
strong
symplectic manifold to infinite dimensional situation which need not admit decomposition into symplectic leaves. If
(M_{1},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{1})
and
(M_{2},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{2})
are BLP spaces, then a smooth function
w:M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
is called a
Poisson map
iff

\{f\circ w,k\circ w\}_{1}=\{f,k\}_{2}\circ w\;\;\forall f,k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M_{2};\mathbb{K}).

(45)

As shown in
[
237
]
, the condition iii) above makes (
45
) equivalent to

\mathfrak{X}_{k}\circ w=\mathbf{T}w\circ\mathfrak{X}_{k\circ w}\;\;\forall k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M_{2};\mathbb{K}).

(46)

If
(M,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
is a BLP space and
h\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{K})
, then the
Hamilton equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f(w^{h}_{t}(x))=\{h,f(w^{h}_{t})\}(x)\;\;\forall f\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(M;\mathbb{K})\;\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\;\;\forall x\in M

(47)

determines a unique
local
map
w^{h}_{t}:M\rightarrow M
, called a
hamiltonian flow
of
h
, which is a Poisson map. The solutions of the equation
x(t)=w^{h}_{t}(x)
with
x(0)=x
need not exist
globally
, that is, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and all
x\in M
. If they exist globally, then the hamiltonian vector field
\{\cdot,h\}
is called
complete
.

2.1.2
W
∗
-algebra predual as a BLP space

If
M
is a Banach space, and a Banach smooth manifold modelled on itself by means of an identity mapping then for each
x\in M
there is a Banach space isomorphism
\mathbf{T}_{x}M\cong M
. If
\mathcal{N}
is a W
∗
-algebra, then the Banach Lie algebra structure of
(\mathcal{N}_{\star})^{\star}\cong\mathcal{N}
is given by its commutator
[x,y]:=xy-yx
, while
\mathrm{ad}_{x}:=[x,\,\cdot\,]=\mathfrak{L}_{x}-\mathfrak{R}_{x}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{x}^{\star}=\mathfrak{L}^{\star}_{x}-\mathfrak{R}^{\star}_{x}
are defined by weakly-
\star
continuous maps
\mathfrak{L}_{x}:\mathcal{N}\ni y\mapsto xy\in\mathcal{N}
,
\mathfrak{R}_{x}:\mathcal{N}\ni y\mapsto yx\in\mathcal{N}
. The condition
\mathrm{ad}_{x}^{\star}(\mathcal{N}_{\star})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}
holds for all
x\in\mathcal{N}
, so
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
is a BLP space that is a holomorphic Banach Poisson manifold (modelled on itself by the atlas consisting of one chart, an identity mapping
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}}
) with the Poisson structure given by (
40
),

\{f,k\}(\phi)=\phi([\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}f,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}k])\;\;\forall f,k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star};\mathbb{C})\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}.

(48)

As a result, the hamiltonian vector field associated to every
k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star};\mathbb{C})
by means of (
41
) takes a form

\mathfrak{X}_{f}(\phi)=-\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}f}(\phi)=\mathfrak{L}^{\star}_{\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}f}(\phi)-\mathfrak{R}^{\star}_{\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}f}(\phi)\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}.

(49)

These results, including the BLP space structure of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
, were discovered by Bóna
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
in the
\mathcal{N}_{\star}=\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})={\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}_{\star}
case, and were generalised to arbitrary W
∗
-algebras by Odzijewicz and Ratiu
[
253
]
. We will call (
49
) the
Bóna--Odzijewicz--Ratiu equation
. If
\mathcal{N}={\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
then
\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cong\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
and for every
\rho\in\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
and every
x,y\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}

\left[\!\left[y,-\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{x}(\rho)\right]\!\right]_{{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}\times{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\star}}=-\left[\!\left[[x,y],\rho\right]\!\right]_{{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}\times{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\star}}=-\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}([x,y]\rho)=\left[\!\left[[x,\rho],y\right]\!\right]_{\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\times{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}},

(50)

which follows from the fact that
\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
is an ideal in
{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
. As a result,

-\mathrm{ad}_{x}^{\star}(\rho)=[x,\rho],

(51)

and the BOR equation (
49
) turns to the Lax equation
[
224
]

\mathfrak{X}_{f}(\rho)=[\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\rho}f,\rho]\;\;\forall\rho\in\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H}).

(52)

In particular, a choice of the Hamilton function
h(\rho):=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}(H\rho)
, where
H\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
but is not necessarily self-adjoint, turns (
47
) to

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\rho(t)=-\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\rho}h}(\rho)=[H,\rho].

(53)

2.1.3
Quantum Poisson systems

The above equation is derived for a Poisson structure on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}=\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
viewed as a holomorphic Banach Poisson manifold. However, the standard construction of unitary dynamics in quantum mechanics makes us to be more interested in the real Banach Poisson manifold
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
(and submanifolds of it that are also subsets of
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
), equipped with the Poisson structure coinduced by the action of the Lie algebra
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}}
of anti-selfadjoint elements of
\mathcal{N}
. More precisely, the set
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}}
of all unitary elements of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
is a real Banach Lie group and has a real Lie Banach algebra
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}})=\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}}:=\{x\in\mathcal{N}\mid x=-x^{*}\}
with
[x,y]:=xy-yx

[
43
]
. The elements of the Banach Lie algebra
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}}
can be represented by
x\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}=\mathrm{i}\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}}
, using the Lie bracket
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\times\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\mathrm{i}[x,y]\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, which corresponds to the commutator
[\mathrm{i}x,\mathrm{i}y]=\mathrm{i}z
in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}}
. This algebra has a unique Banach predual, given by
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\cong L_{1}(\mathcal{N})^{\mathrm{sa}}:=\{\phi\in L_{1}(\mathcal{N})\mid\phi=\phi^{*}\}
, with an isomorphism
(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}})^{\star}\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}
defined by duality

\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\times\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\ni(\phi,x)\mapsto\left[\!\left[\phi,x\right]\!\right]_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\times\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}}:=\phi(x)\in\mathbb{R}.

(54)

The adjoint representation
\mathrm{Ad}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}})
of a Banach Lie group
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}}
on
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}})=\mathrm{i}\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}=\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}}
,

\mathrm{Ad}(u)x:=uxu^{*}\;\;\forall u\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}}\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}

(55)

determine the coadjoint representation
\mathrm{Ad}^{\star}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}})
on
(\mathcal{N}^{\star})^{\mathrm{sa}}
,

\left[\!\left[x,\mathrm{Ad}^{\star}(u)\phi\right]\!\right]_{\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\times(\mathcal{N}^{\star})^{\mathrm{sa}}}:=\left[\!\left[\mathrm{Ad}(u^{-1})x,\phi\right]\!\right]_{\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\times(\mathcal{N}^{\star})^{\mathrm{sa}}}\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\;\forall\phi\in(\mathcal{N}^{\star})^{\mathrm{sa}}.

(56)

Using these properties, one can show that
[
38
,
253
]

\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{x}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\subseteq(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}})^{\star}\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}}.

(57)

The space
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
can be equipped with a real Banach smooth manifold structure modelled on itself by the atlas consisting of one chart, which is determined by the identity mapping on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
. As a result,
\mathbf{T}_{\phi}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}})\cong\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}

\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
. So, it is possible to use (
40
) and (
54
) to
define
the BLP structure on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
by

\{f,k\}(\phi):=\mathrm{i}\phi([\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}f,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}k])\;\;\forall f,k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}};\mathbb{R})\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}.

(58)

As a result, the Hamilton equation (
47
) for
h\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}};\mathbb{R})
reads

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f(\phi(t))=\{h,f\}(\phi(t))=\mathrm{i}(\phi(t))\left([\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi(t)}h,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi(t)}f]\right).

(59)

The spaces
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 1}
,
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
and
(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}})
are subsets of
(\mathcal{N}^{\star})^{\mathrm{sa}}
that are invariant with respect to
\mathrm{Ad}^{\star}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}})
. As shown in
[
27
]
, they decompose into union of orbits of
\mathrm{Ad}^{\star}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}})
, which in turn are weak symplectic manifolds, which provides the symplectic foliation of the BLP space
(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}})
. Similarly,
(\mathcal{N}_{\star},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}})
is invariant with respect to the action of the Banach Lie group
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{inv}}
of all invertible elements of
\mathcal{N}
. If
\mathcal{N}={\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
and
\rho\in\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{\mathrm{sa}}
, then the calculation analogous to (
50
) gives

\mathrm{ad}_{x}^{\star}(\rho)=[\rho,x]\;\;\forall x\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\mathrm{asa}}\;\forall\rho\in\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{\mathrm{sa}}.

(60)

As a result, the BOR equation (
49
) on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}=\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{\mathrm{sa}}
takes the form

\mathfrak{X}_{f}(\rho)=[\rho,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\rho}f]\;\;\forall\rho\in\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H}),

(61)

while the Hamilton equation (
59
) becomes
[
38
]

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f(\rho(t))=\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}\left([\rho(t),\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\rho(t)}h]\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\rho(t)}f\right).

(62)

Because of the identity

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f(\rho(t))=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}\left((\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\rho(t)}f)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\rho(t)\right),

(63)

the equation (
62
) is equivalent to the
Bóna equation

[
36
,
187
,
38
]
,

\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\rho(t)=[\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\rho(t)}h,\rho(t)].

(64)

The solutions of (
64
) are state-dependent unitary operators
U(\rho,t)
. They do not form a group, but satisfy a cocycle relationship:

U(\rho,t+s)=U((\mathrm{Ad}(U(\rho,t)))(\rho),s)U(\rho,t)\;\;\forall t,s\in\mathbb{R}.

(65)

In the special case, when
h(\rho)=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}(\rho H)
for
H\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\mathrm{sa}}=\mathrm{i}{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\mathrm{asa}}
, (
64
) turns to the
von Neumann equation

\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\rho(t)=[H,\rho(t)].

(66)

So far we have followed the Bóna–Odzijewicz–Ratiu approach, hence our main object of interest was the real Banach manifold
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}_{\star}
, equipped with the BLP space structure coinduced by the Banach–Lie algebra
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{asa}}
via
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, corresponding to the group
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}}
of unitary elements of
\mathcal{N}
. However, in principle, a geometric setting for nonlinear dynamics of quantum models can be generated by an arbitrary Banach Lie algebra
\mathcal{B}
over
\mathbb{R}
such that:

(i)

its Banach predual space
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
exists, is unique, and is a real Banach Poisson manifold,

(ii)

\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{x}(\mathcal{B}_{\star})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\star}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\star}

\forall x\in\mathcal{B}
,

(iii)

there exists a nonempty set
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
that is a real BLP submanifold of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
.

In what follows, we will call such Banach Lie algebras
\mathcal{B}
to be
well-adapted
. For any choice of
h\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B});\mathbb{R})
, the pair
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}),h)
will be called a
quantum Poisson system
whenever

w^{h}_{t}(\phi)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})\;\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

(67)

Hence, each quantum Poisson system is determined by a choice of: a space of quantum states
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
, a Banach Lie algebra
\mathcal{B}
, a tangent bundle (real Banach manifold) structure on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, and a real Fréchet smooth function on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, satisfying the conditions (i), (ii), (iii), and (
67
). The assumptions
\mathcal{B}\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}
and
\mathbf{T}_{\phi}\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}_{\star}:=\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}

\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}_{\star}
recover the BOR setting completely. Note that a general quantum Poisson system
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}),h)
does not have to be related to any group, so in particular to a group of unitary operators
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{uni}}
. The only shared property (securing the backwards compatibility with quantum mechanical setting) is implementation of the Poisson flow on the predual by means of a coinduced action of a Banach–Lie algebra.

2.2
Relative modular operators, standard liouvilleans and the GNS bundle

A
weight
on a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
is defined as a function
\omega:\mathcal{N}^{+}\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
such that
\omega(0)=0
,
\omega(x+y)=\omega(x)+\omega(y)
, and
\lambda\geq 0\Rightarrow\omega(\lambda x)=\lambda\omega(x)
, with the convention
0\cdot(+\infty)=0
. A weight is called:
faithful
iff
\omega(x)=0\Rightarrow x=0
;
finite
iff
\omega(\mathbb{I})<\infty
;
semi-finite
iff a left ideal in
\mathcal{N}
given by
\mathfrak{n}_{\phi}:=\{x\in\mathcal{N}\mid\phi(x^{*}x)<\infty\}
is weakly-
\star
dense in
\mathcal{N}
;
normal
iff
\omega(\sup\{x_{\iota}\})=\sup\{\omega(x_{\iota})\}
for any uniformly bounded increasing net
\{x_{\iota}\}\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}
. A space of all normal semi-finite weights on a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
is denoted
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{N})
, while the subset of all faithful elements of
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{N})
is denoted
\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
. Hence,
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\subset\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathcal{N}_{\star 0}^{+}\subset\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
. For
\psi\in\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{N})
,
\mathrm{supp}(\psi)=\mathbb{I}-\sup\{P\in\mathrm{Proj}(\mathcal{N})\mid\psi(P)=0\}
. An element
\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
is faithful iff
\mathrm{supp}(\omega)=\mathbb{I}
.

A
representation
of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
is defined as a pair
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and a
*
-homomorphism
\pi:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
. A representation
\pi:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
is called:
nondegenerate
iff
\{\pi(x)\xi\mid(x,\xi)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{H}\}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
;
normal
iff it is continuous with respect to the weak-
\star
topologies of
\mathcal{N}
and
{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
;
faithful
iff
\ker(\pi)=\{0\}
. An element
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
is called
cyclic
for a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}\subseteq{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
iff
\mathcal{N}\xi:=\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{N}}\{x\xi\}
is norm dense in
{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
. A representation
\pi:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
is called
cyclic
iff there exists
\Omega\in\mathcal{H}
that is cyclic for
\pi(\mathcal{N})
. According to the Gel’fand–Naĭmark–Segal theorem
[
106
,
311
]
for every pair
(\mathcal{N},\omega)
of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
and
\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{\star+}
there exists a triple
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\Omega_{\omega})
of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
and a cyclic representation
\pi_{\omega}:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
with a cyclic vector
\Omega_{\omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, and this triple is unique up to unitary equivalence. An analogue of this theorem for weights follows the similar construction, but lacks cyclicity. If
\omega
is a weight on a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
, then there exists the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, defined as the completion of
\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}/\ker(\omega)
in the topology of a norm generated by the scalar product
\left\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\right\rangle_{\omega}:\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}\times\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\omega(x^{*}y)\in\mathbb{C}
,

\mathcal{H}_{\omega}:=\overline{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}/\ker(\omega)}=\overline{\{x\in\mathcal{N}\mid\omega(x^{*}x)<\infty\}/\{x\in\mathcal{N}\mid\omega(x^{*}x)=0\}}=\overline{\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}/\mathcal{I}_{\omega}},

(68)

and there exist the maps

\displaystyle[\cdot]_{\omega}:\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}\ni x

\displaystyle\mapsto[x]_{\omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega},

(69)

\displaystyle\pi_{\omega}:\mathcal{N}\ni x

\displaystyle\mapsto([y]_{\omega}\mapsto[xy]_{\omega})\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega}),

(70)

such that
[\cdot]_{\omega}
is linear,
\mathrm{ran}([\cdot]_{\omega})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, and
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega})
is a representation of
\mathcal{N}
. If
\omega\in\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{N})
then
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega})
is nondegenerate and normal. It is also faithful if
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
.

A standard representation
[
130
]
of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
is defined as a quadruple
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, a nondegenerate faithful weakly-
\star
continuous representation
\pi:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
, a conjugation
J:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, and a self-polar cone
20
20

20

A subspace
\mathcal{D}
of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is called a self-polar cone iff
\lambda\xi\in\mathcal{D}

\forall\xi\in\mathcal{D}

\forall\lambda\geq 0
and
\mathcal{D}=\{\zeta\in\mathcal{H}\mid\left\langle{\xi,\zeta}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\geq 0\;\forall\xi\in\mathcal{D}\}
.

\mathcal{H}^{\natural}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, satisfying the conditions

J\pi(\mathcal{N})J=\pi(\mathcal{N})^{\bullet},\;\;\xi\in\mathcal{H}^{\natural}\;\Rightarrow\;J\xi=\xi,\;\;\pi(x)J\pi(x)\mathcal{H}^{\natural}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\natural},\;\;y\in{\mathfrak{Z}}(\pi(\mathcal{N}))\;\Rightarrow\;JyJ=y^{*}.

(71)

For any standard representation

\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}\;\;\exists!{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi)\in\mathcal{H}^{\natural}\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}\;\;\phi(x)=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi),\pi(x){\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi)}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

(72)

The map
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}:\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\natural}
is order preserving. Moreover,
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\pi}:\mathcal{H}^{\natural}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
, defined by
({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\pi}(\xi))(x)=\left\langle{\xi,\pi(x)\xi}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\forall x\in\mathcal{N}
, is a bijective norm continuous homomorphism with
({\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\pi})^{-1}={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}
. For any two standard representations
(\mathcal{H}_{1},\pi_{1},J_{1},\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{1})
and
(\mathcal{H}_{2},\pi_{2},J_{2},\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{2})
of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
and a given
∗
-isomorphism
\varsigma:\pi_{1}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\pi_{2}(\mathcal{N})
, there exists a unique unitary
u_{\varsigma}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that
\varsigma(x)=u_{\varsigma}xu_{\varsigma}^{*}

\forall x\in\pi_{1}(\mathcal{N})
,
J_{2}=u_{\varsigma}J_{1}u_{\varsigma}^{*}
,
\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{2}=u_{\varsigma}\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{1}
. Such
u_{\varsigma}
will be called a standard unitary equivalence. If
\phi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
then, by means of the Tomita–Takesaki theory
[
350
,
347
]
, the GNS representation associated with
\phi
determines a unique conjugation
J_{\phi}
, and a weakly-
\star
continuous group homomorphism
\sigma^{\omega}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
. An associated
\mathcal{H}^{\natural}
is given by
[
73
,
18
]

\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{\phi}:=\overline{\bigcup_{x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\phi}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{\phi}^{*}}\{\pi_{\phi}(x)J_{\phi}\pi_{\phi}(x)J_{\phi}\Omega_{\phi}\}}^{\mathcal{H}_{\phi}}.

(73)

If
\mathcal{N}\cong\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{K})
for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
is given by
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{K}}
, then the corresponding GNS Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
is given by the space of all Hilbert–Schmidt operators,

\mathfrak{G}_{2}(\mathcal{K}):=\{x\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{K})\mid(\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{K}}(x^{*}x))^{1/2}<\infty\}=\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{K}}},

(74)

equipped with a scalar product
\left\langle{x,y}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{G}_{2}(\mathcal{K})}:=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{K}}(x^{*}y)
, so that
\mathfrak{G}_{2}(\mathcal{K})\cong\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{K}^{\star}
as Hilbert spaces. Moreover,
\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{\phi}=\mathfrak{G}_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{+}
,
\pi_{\phi}(x)=\mathfrak{L}_{x}
(which denotes left multiplication by
x
), while
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}:\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{K})^{+}\ni\rho\mapsto\rho^{1/2}\in\mathfrak{G}_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{+}
.
For a given W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
,
\phi\in\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{N})
, and
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
the map

R_{\phi,\omega}:[x]_{\omega}\mapsto[x^{*}]_{\phi}\;\;\forall x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{\phi}^{*}

(75)

is a densely defined, closable antilinear operator. Its closure admits a unique polar decomposition

\overline{R}_{\phi,\omega}=J_{\phi,\omega}\Delta^{1/2}_{\phi,\omega},

(76)

where
J_{\phi,\omega}
is a conjugation operator, called
relative modular conjugation
, while
\Delta_{\phi,\omega}
is a positive self-adjoint operator on
\mathrm{dom}(\Delta_{\phi,\omega})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
with
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta_{\phi,\omega})=\mathrm{supp}(\phi)\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, called a
relative modular operator

[
17
,
73
,
82
]
. Given
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
,
\Delta_{\omega,\omega}=:\Delta_{\omega}
implements the action of the Tomita–Takesaki (modular) automorphism
\sigma^{\omega}
by

\pi_{\omega}(\sigma^{\omega}_{t}(x))=\Delta_{\omega}^{\mathrm{i}t}\pi_{\omega}(x)\Delta_{\omega}^{-\mathrm{i}t}.

(77)

If
\mathcal{N}\cong{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
,
\phi=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}(\rho_{\phi}\,\cdot)
,
\omega=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}(\rho_{\omega}\,\cdot)
, and
\mathfrak{R}_{x}
denotes right multiplication by
x\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
, then
\Delta_{\phi,\omega}=\mathfrak{L}_{\rho_{\phi}}\mathfrak{R}_{\rho_{\omega}^{-1}}
.

For every
\phi,\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
the relative modular conjugation
J_{\phi,\omega}
determines a unique unitary operator
J_{\phi,\phi}J_{\phi,\omega}=:V_{\phi,\omega}:\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
, such that

\displaystyle\pi_{\phi}(x)

\displaystyle=V_{\phi,\omega}\pi_{\omega}(x)V_{\phi,\omega}^{*},

(78)

\displaystyle V_{\phi,\omega}(\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{\omega})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{\phi},

(79)

\displaystyle V_{\phi,\omega}J_{\omega,\omega}

\displaystyle=J_{\phi,\phi}V_{\phi,\omega}.

(80)

We will call
V_{\phi,\omega}

standard unitary transition
between
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
. It is a standard unitary equivalence of a
∗
-isomorphism
\varsigma_{\phi,\omega}:\pi_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\pi_{\phi}(\mathcal{N})
determined by the condition
\varsigma_{\phi,\omega}\circ\pi_{\omega}=\pi_{\phi}
. Thus, if
\phi,\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, then
V_{\phi,\omega}
provides a default unitary mapping between the corresponding GNS Hilbert spaces and representations.

Given any group
G
, a
representation
of
G
in the group
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
of
*
-automorphisms of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
is a map
\alpha:G\ni g\mapsto\alpha(g)=:\alpha_{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
which is a
group homomorphism
, that is,

1)

\alpha(e)=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{N}}
,

2)

\alpha(g_{1})\circ\alpha(g_{2})=\alpha(g_{1}\circ g_{2})

\forall g_{1},g_{2}\in G
,

where
e
denotes the neutral element of
G
. A group
G
is called:
topological
iff it is also a topological space and a map
G\times G\ni(g_{1},g_{2})\mapsto g_{1}\circ g_{2}^{-1}\in G
is continuous for all
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
;
locally compact
iff it is topological and
e\in G
has a compact topological neighbourhood. For any W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
,
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
is a topological group with respect to
weak-
\star
topology
on
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
, defined by the collection of neighbourhoods
[
348
]

N_{\{\omega_{i}\}}(\alpha):=\{\varsigma\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})\mid{\left|\!\left|{\omega_{i}\circ\alpha-\omega_{i}\circ\varsigma}\right|\!\right|}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}}<1,\;{\left|\!\left|{\omega_{i}\circ\alpha^{-1}-\omega_{i}\circ\varsigma^{-1}}\right|\!\right|}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}}<1\},

(81)

where
\{\omega_{i}\}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A triple
(\mathcal{N},G,\alpha)
of a W
∗
-algebra, locally compact group
G
, and a representation
\alpha:G\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
is called a
W
∗
-dynamical system
(or a
W
∗
-covariant system
) iff
\alpha
is continuous in the weak-
\star
topology of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
. This condition is equivalent to the continuity of the map
G\ni g\mapsto\alpha_{g}(x)\in\mathcal{N}
in the weak-
\star
topology of
\mathcal{N}
for any
x\in\mathcal{N}
, that is, to

G\ni g\mapsto\phi(\alpha_{g}(x))\in\mathbb{C}\mbox{ is a continuous function}\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N},

(82)

and such
\alpha
is called a
weakly-
\star
continuous
representation. Uniqueness of a predual of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
allows to define isometries
\alpha_{\star}
of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
that uniquely correspond to the elements
\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
, and to define the isometries of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
uniquely corresponding to representations
\alpha:G\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
:

\left[\!\left[(\alpha_{g})_{\star}(\phi),x\right]\!\right]_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}\times\mathcal{N}}=\left[\!\left[\phi,\alpha_{g}(x)\right]\!\right]_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}\times\mathcal{N}}=\phi(\alpha_{g}(x))\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}.

(83)

The above equivalence can be shown (see e.g.
[
295
]
) by proving that (
82
) implies continuity of
\alpha_{\star}
in the norm of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
,

\lim_{g\rightarrow e}{\left|\!\left|{(\alpha_{g})_{\star}(\phi)-\phi}\right|\!\right|}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}}=0\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}\;\forall g\in G.

(84)

A
unitary implementation
of a representation
\alpha:G\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
in a given representation
\pi:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
is defined as a map
u:G\ni g\mapsto u(g)\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\mathrm{uni}}
that determines a family
\{u(g)\mid g\in G\}
of unitary operators satisfying the
covariance equation

\pi(\alpha_{g}(x))=u(g)\pi(x)u(g)^{*}\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}\;\forall g\in G.

(85)

The condition (
85
) alone does not determine
\{u(g)\mid g\in G\}
uniquely. The setting of W
∗
-algebras admits a remarkable solution to this problem: every pair of a W
∗
-dynamical system
(\mathcal{N},\mathbb{R},\alpha)
and a standard representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
determines uniquely a corresponding unitary implementation
together with
a unique self-adjoint generator of this family of unitaries. This generator is called a
standard liouvillean
21
21

21

It would be however more precise to call it
quantum koopmanian
, because in the commutative setting (of statistical mechanics and probability measures) the ‘liouvillean operator’ (defined by the Poisson bracket) acts on elements of
L_{1}(\mathcal{X},\mho(\mathcal{X}),{\tilde{\mu}})
, while it is the ‘koopmanian operator’
[
201
,
363
,
364
]
that acts on the positive cone of
L_{1}(\mathcal{X},\mho(\mathcal{X}),{\tilde{\mu}})
.
. It is not called ‘hamiltonian’, because in general its spectrum may be not bounded from any side, while the notion of ‘hamiltonian’ is usually understood as referring to a self-adjoint operator that generates a strongly continuous group of unitary operators
and
has a nonnegative (or at least bounded from below) spectrum
22
22

22

E.g., «one of the most important principles of quantum field theory, ensuring the stability, demands that the energy should have a lower bound»
[
127
]
.
. Moreover, as opposed to hamiltonian, the construction of standard liouvillean for a given W
∗
-dynamical system does not require any additional analytic conditions that constrain derivation to an ‘integrable’ infinitesimal generator. This way the W
∗
-algebraic approach makes the notion of a hamiltonian less relevant than the notion of a liouvillean.

For any W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
, the unique predualisation of action of
\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
can be connected with the uniqueness property of representation of elements of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
in terms of a standard cone of a standard representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
of
\mathcal{N}
: any
\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
defines a unique map
u:\mathcal{H}^{\natural}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\natural}
by

u{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi):={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\alpha_{\star}(\phi))\;\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}.

(86)

This map is linear, can be extended to a unitary operator on all
\mathcal{H}
, and satisfies

u\pi(x)u^{*}=\pi(\alpha(x))\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}.

(87)

This leads to a question, whether it is possible to generate this way a
standard
unitary implementation of a given representation
\alpha:G\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
. The answer is in the affirmative, and was established by Haagerup
[
130
]
(the special cases of this result were obtained earlier in
[
188
,
189
,
140
,
133
,
267
]
). If
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
is a standard representation of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
, then there exists a unique strongly continuous unitary implementation
V_{\alpha}(g)
of
\alpha
satisfying

\displaystyle V_{\alpha}(g)\mathcal{H}^{\natural}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{\natural},

(88)

\displaystyle JV_{\alpha}(g)

\displaystyle=V_{\alpha}(g)J.

(89)

Such family
\{V_{\alpha}(g)\mid g\in G\}
is called a
standard
unitary implementation of
\alpha
.

Thus, if
(\mathcal{N},\mathbb{R},\alpha)
is a W
∗
-dynamical system with
\mathcal{N}
in standard form
(\mathcal{H},\pi(\mathcal{N}),J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
, then from the theorems of Haagerup and Stone
[
335
,
336
,
362
]
it follows that there exists a unique strongly continuous group of unitaries
\{V_{\alpha}(t)\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}\subseteq{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\mathrm{uni}}
, and a unique self-adjoint operator
K^{\alpha}
on
\mathcal{H}
, called
standard liouvillean
, such that
V_{\alpha}(t)
is a strongly continuous unitary implementation of
\alpha
and for every
t\in\mathbb{R}

i)

V_{\alpha}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tK^{\alpha}}
,

ii)

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tK^{\alpha}}\mathcal{H}^{\natural}=\mathcal{H}^{\natural}
,

iii)

JK^{\alpha}+K^{\alpha}J=0
.

The definition of a standard liouvillean
K^{\alpha}
does not depend on any choice of
\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
or
\omega\in\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{N})
: it depends only on a W
∗
-dynamical system and a standard representation of W
∗
-algebra. If
\mathcal{N}
is semi-finite,
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
is its standard representation,
(\mathcal{N},\mathbb{R},\alpha)
is a W
∗
-dynamical system,
H\in\pi(\mathcal{N})^{\mathrm{sa}}
, and
\{U(t):=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}\in\pi(\mathcal{N})\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}
is a strongly continuous group of unitary operators such that

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\pi(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}=\pi(\alpha_{t}(x))\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R},

(90)

then the standard liouvillean reads
[
44
,
165
]

K^{\alpha}=H-JHJ=[H,\,\cdot\,].

(91)

Following Odzijewicz and Sliżewska
[
256
]
, consider a bundle
\mathfrak{e}:V\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
, where

V:=\{(x,\omega)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\mid x\,\mathrm{supp}(\omega)=x\},

(92)

and the bundle projection
\mathfrak{e}
is given by a restriction of the cartesian product projection
\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
to
V
. Because
V_{\omega}:=\mathfrak{e}^{-1}(\omega)=\mathcal{N}\mathrm{supp}(\omega)

\forall\omega\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
, the scalar product

V_{\omega}\times V_{\omega}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\left\langle{x,y}\right\rangle_{\omega}:=\left[\!\left[\omega,x^{*}y\right]\!\right]_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}\times\mathcal{N}}\in\mathbb{C}

(93)

is nondegenerate. Moreover,
\left\langle{x,x}\right\rangle_{\omega}=0

\iff

x\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{I}-\mathrm{supp}(\omega))
. The completion
\bar{V}_{\omega}
of
V_{\omega}
under the norm generated by
\left\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\right\rangle_{\omega}
determines a bundle
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}:=\bar{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
of Hilbert spaces, which the authors of
[
256
]
call the
Gel’fand--Naĭmark--Segal bundle
(the notion of the GNS bundle was earlier alluded in
[
164
,
66
]
).

While (
73
) secures that the GNS representation is a standard representation whenever
\phi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, it doesn’t have to be in more general case. Thus, in order to be sure that our use of GNS bundle coincides with the necessary conditions for Haagerup’s theorem, we will restrict our discussion in multiple places of this paper to subsets and submanifolds of
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
. We consider this restriction to be nonoptimal, but in order to work it out in larger generality, we would have to work with a different bundle of Hilbert spaces. Restriction to
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
allows us to use standard unitary transitions
V_{\phi,\omega}
to map between Hilbert spaces, at the expense of consideration of unitarily equivalent representations only. Whenever the assumption of restriction to
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
is made, it implies restriction of considerations to countably finite W
∗
-algebras, because only for them
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}\neq\varnothing
.

2.3
Case study: Algebraic hamiltonian vector fields

The BLP structure of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
allows to introduce and analyse the temporal evolution on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
by means of the hamiltonian vector field and the Hamilton equation. On the other hand, for any W
∗
-dynamical system
(\mathcal{N},\mathbb{R},\alpha)
one can predualise the representation
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\alpha_{t}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
obtaining the family
\{\alpha_{\star}^{t}\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}
of norm continuous isometries
\alpha_{\star}^{t}:=(\alpha_{t})_{\star}:\mathcal{N}_{\star}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}
, which in turn can be analysed by means of a unique self-adjoint standard liouvillean operator
K^{\alpha}
that generates a unitary evolution in
L_{2}(\mathcal{N})
leaving
L_{2}(\mathcal{N})^{+}
invariant. The virtue of a geometric description in terms of hamiltonian vector field is that it allows for an analysis of the local differential structure of temporal evolution in terms of a local Poisson flow and tangent space. However, it does not guarantee the existence of global flow. On the other hand, an algebraic description in terms of a predualised representation
\alpha_{\star}
and an associated standard liouvillean
K^{\alpha}
guarantees the existence of a global flow on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
, but it is not necessarily a Poisson flow and it is not related to a tangent space, thus it does not allow (in general) for a refined smooth geometric description. This leads us to single out the class of evolutions on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
(and
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
) that satisfy both conditions.

The isometries
\alpha_{\star}^{t}
of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
that are also the Poisson flows leaving
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
invariant are characterised as solutions of the equation (
45
)

\{f\circ\alpha_{\star}^{t},k\circ\alpha_{\star}^{t}\}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}}=\{f,k\}_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}}\circ\alpha_{\star}^{t}\;\;\forall f,k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}};\mathbb{R})\;\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R},

(94)

which gives, by (
58
),

\displaystyle\phi([\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}(f\circ\alpha_{\star}^{t}),\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}(k\circ\alpha_{\star}^{t})])=(\alpha_{\star}^{t}(\phi))([\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\alpha_{\star}^{t}(\phi)},\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\alpha^{t}_{\star}(\phi)}k]),

(95)

\displaystyle 0=\phi([\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}(f\circ\alpha_{\star}^{t}),\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}(k\circ\alpha_{\star}^{t})]-\alpha_{t}([\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\alpha_{\star}^{t}(\phi)}f,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\alpha_{\star}^{t}(\phi)}k])).

(96)

Hence, the predualisation
\alpha_{\star}
of a weakly-
\star
continuous representation
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
is a Poisson flow on
(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}},\{\cdot,\cdot\})
iff
\alpha
satisfies

\phi((\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{N}}-\alpha_{t})([\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\alpha_{\star}^{t}(\phi)}f,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\alpha_{\star}^{t}(\phi)}k]))=0\;\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\;\;\forall f,k\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}};\mathbb{R})\;\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

(97)

We will call (
97
) the
Poisson compatibility condition
(PC
1
). Let
(\mathcal{N},\mathbb{R},\alpha)
be a W
∗
-dynamical system satisfying the Poisson compatibility condition. Then the Poisson flow
\alpha_{\star}|_{\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}}
is generated by the Hamilton function
h^{\alpha}\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}};\mathbb{R})
according to (
47
),

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f_{t}=\{h^{\alpha},f_{t}\},\;\;f_{t}(x):=f(\alpha^{t}_{\star}(x))\;\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}\;\forall f\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}};\mathbb{R}),

(98)

which determines the corresponding unique hamiltonian vector field
\mathfrak{X}_{h^{\alpha}}\in\mathbf{T}\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
by means of the BOR equation (
49
),

\mathfrak{X}_{h^{\alpha}}(\phi)=-\mathrm{ad}^{\star}_{\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}h^{\alpha}}(\phi)=\mathfrak{L}^{\star}_{\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}h^{\alpha}}(\phi)-\mathfrak{R}^{\star}_{\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}h^{\alpha}}(\phi)\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}.

(99)

We will call
\mathfrak{X}_{h^{\alpha}}
an
algebraic hamiltonian vector field
.

Now, let us recall from Section
2.2
that, by the Haagerup theorem, each pair of a W
∗
-dynamical system
(\mathcal{N},\mathbb{R},\alpha)
and a standard representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
of
\mathcal{N}
determines a unique self-adjoint generator
K^{\alpha}
of a unitary implementation of
\alpha
in
{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
satisfying the condition
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tK^{\alpha}}\mathcal{H}^{\natural}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\natural}
. By means of (
72
), this condition expresses the requirement that
\alpha_{\star}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
(if formulated in terms of Kosaki’s canonical representation, for which
\mathcal{H}^{\natural}=L_{2}(\mathcal{N})^{+}
, this condition is just an
L_{2}(\mathcal{N})
version of
L_{1}(\mathcal{N})^{+}=\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
invariance under
\alpha
). If
\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, then the GNS representation,
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\Omega_{\omega})
is also a standard representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},J_{\omega},\mathcal{H}^{+}_{\omega})
, so we can apply Haagerup’s theorem to the fibres of the GNS bundle
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
restricted to the submanifold
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
,
\mathfrak{e}:\mathcal{H}\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
. Because
\mathrm{supp}(\omega)=\mathbb{I}
for each
\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, the bundle projection
\mathfrak{e}
reduces in this case to a cartesian product projection. Orbits of any Poisson flow leave
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
invariant
[
38
,
27
,
287
]
, while
\alpha_{\star}^{t}
is norm preserving, so the restrictions of Poisson compatible isometries
\alpha^{t}_{\star}
to
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
are automorphisms of this space. As a result, we obtain a remarkable geometric correspondence: every weakly-
\star
continuous representation
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\alpha_{t}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
satisfying the Poisson compatibility condition (
97
) determines a unique globally integrable hamiltonian vector field
\mathfrak{X}_{h^{\alpha}}\in\mathbf{T}\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}

and
a family of standard liouvillean operators
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}\ni\omega\mapsto K_{\omega}^{\alpha}\in(\mathrm{Lin}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega}))^{\mathrm{sa}}
acting pointwise on the GNS bundle of Hilbert spaces. In other words, the family
\{\alpha_{t}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}
of Poisson compatible, weakly-
\star
continuous automorphisms of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
is uniquely represented
in
the tangent vector bundle
\mathbf{T}\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
(and, by linearity, also in
\mathbf{T}\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
), as well as
on
the GNS Hilbert bundle
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
.

Due to uniqueness property (
72
) of the embedding
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}:\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\ni\omega\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\omega)\in\mathcal{H}^{\natural}
of any standard representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
, this means that the embedding of a trajectory generated by
\alpha_{\star}^{t}
on
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
for any
\phi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
coincides with the evolution in
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}^{+}
generated by
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tK_{\phi}^{\alpha}}
. Hence, the hamiltonian flow
w_{t}^{h^{\alpha}}
of
\mathfrak{X}_{h^{\alpha}}
on
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
from
\phi(0)
to
\phi(t)
can be always represented as liouvillean evolution

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tK_{\phi(t)}^{\alpha}}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\phi(t)}(\phi(0))={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\phi(t)}(\phi(t))=:\Omega_{\phi(t)},

(100)

where by
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\psi}(\omega)
we denote the standard representative of
\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
in the positive cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{\psi}
of the GNS representation Hilbert space of
\psi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
.

If
\mathcal{N}={\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
,
\rho\in\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{+}_{0}
, and the weakly-
\star
continuous representation
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}({\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})})
is unitary (that is,
\alpha_{t}=\mathrm{Ad}(u(t))
with
u(t)\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\mathrm{uni}}

\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
), then the algebraic hamiltonian vector field can be expressed by the von Neumann equation (
66
), while the corresponding liouvillean evolution
\rho(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tK^{\alpha}}\rho(0)
in
\mathfrak{G}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\cong\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\star}
is a solution of the equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\rho(t)=-\mathrm{i}K^{\alpha}\rho(t),

(101)

which gives (
91
).
In this sense, the Poisson compatibility condition (
97
) extends the equivalence between the algebraic (liouvillean operator) and geometric (hamiltonian vector) descriptions of temporal evolution of quantum states to the general W
∗
-dynamical systems. In the next Section we will investigate how standard liouvilleans can be used to encode the perturbation of Poisson flow by additional geometric structures over state space, beyond the realms of W
∗
-dynamical systems.

2.4
Relative entropy, Norden–Sen geometry, and noncommutative Orlicz spaces

2.4.1
Distances, Norden–Sen geometries, and geodesic free falls as entropic projections

A pair
(\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
of two affine connections over a smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}
will be called
Norden--Sen dual
with respect to a riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}
on
\mathcal{M}
, iff
[
313
,
314
,
315
,
249
,
250
,
251
]

\mathbf{g}(\nabla_{u}v,w)+\mathbf{g}(v,\nabla^{\dagger}_{u}w)=u(\mathbf{g}(v,w))\;\forall u,v,w\in\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M},

(102)

which is equivalent to

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{t}_{c}^{\nabla}u,\mathbf{t}_{c}^{\nabla^{\dagger}}v)=\mathbf{g}(u,v)

(103)

for all
u,v\in\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}
and for all curves
c:\mathbb{R}\supset[r_{1},r_{2}]\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
(the symbol
\mathbf{t}_{c}^{\nabla}
denotes a parallel transport along
c
that is determined by an affine connection
\nabla
). The quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
is called a
Norden--Sen geometry
. A riemannian geometry is characterised as a Norden–Sen geometry with
\nabla=\nabla^{\dagger}
.

Given a set
M
, we define a
distance
on
M
as a function
D:M\times M\rightarrow[0,\infty]
such that
D(\phi,\omega)=0

\iff

\omega=\phi
. Eguchi
[
94
,
95
,
96
]
showed that for any smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}
and any smooth distance
D
on
\mathcal{M}
that satisfies

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{v}|_{p}\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{v}|_{p}D(p,q)|_{q=p}\in\,]0,\infty[\;\;\;\forall p\in\mathcal{M}\;\forall v\in\mathbf{T}_{p}\mathcal{M}\setminus\{0\},

(104)

where
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{v}|_{p}
denotes here the Gâteaux derivative at
p\in\mathcal{M}
in the direction
v\in\mathbf{T}_{p}\mathcal{M}
, the distance
D
determines a riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}
and a pair of affine connections
(\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
on
\mathcal{M}
, given by the
Eguchi equations

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\phi}(u,v)

\displaystyle:=-\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{u}|_{\phi}\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{v}|_{\omega}D(\phi,\omega)|_{\omega=\phi},

(105)

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\phi}((\nabla_{u})_{\phi}v,w)

\displaystyle:=-\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{u}|_{\phi}\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{v}|_{\phi}\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{w}|_{\omega}D(\phi,\omega)|_{\omega=\phi},

(106)

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\phi}(v,(\nabla_{u}^{\dagger})_{\phi}w)

\displaystyle:=-\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{u}|_{\omega}\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{w}|_{\omega}\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{v}|_{\phi}D(\phi,\omega)|_{\omega=\phi}.

(107)

Every quadruple
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
determined in this way is a Norden–Sen geometry such that both
\nabla
and
\nabla^{\dagger}
are torsion-free. A torsion-free Norden–Sen geometry will be called an
Eguchi geometry
. While in riemannian geometry the affine connection is determined by the riemannian metric, in the Eguchi geometry the triple of riemannian metric and two Norden–Sen dual affine connections are determined by the distance. The Levi-Civita connection
\bar{\nabla}
of an associated riemannian geometry
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
satisfies
\bar{\nabla}=(\nabla+\nabla^{\dagger})/2
. In this sense, the Eguchi geometry (based on the nonsymmetric distance) provides a generalisation of a riemannian geometry and cartesian geometry, including all of their main notions: distance, length, parallelity and orthogonality. Generalisation of the cartesian distance is provided by the distance
D
, the induced riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}
provides the generalisation of orthogonality and length, while the induced torsion-free Norden–Sen dual connections
(\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
provide a generalisation of parallelity.
23
23

23

The idea that
D
should be considered as generalisation of the cartesian distance, while the connection
\nabla
associated to a projection by means of
D
should be considered as a proper generalisation of parallelity (at least in the setting of statistical manifolds) is due to Chencov
[
53
,
57
]
.
The invariance of length under parallel transport that characterises riemannian geometry is weakened to covariance in the sense of (
103
).

If both affine connections of a Norden–Sen geometry are flat and torsion-free, then it is called a
dually flat geometry

[
246
,
7
,
9
]
. If
\dim\mathcal{M}=:n<\infty
, then every dually flat geometry
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
determines a unique pair of affine immersions
\Psi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\Psi^{\mathbf{L}}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{ij}(\rho(\theta))

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}\Psi(\rho(\theta))}{\partial\theta^{i}\partial\theta^{j}},

(108)

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{ij}(\rho(\eta))

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}\Psi^{\mathbf{L}}(\rho(\eta))}{\partial\eta^{i}\partial\eta^{j}},

(109)

where
\{\theta^{i}\}
is a coordinate system such that
\Gamma^{\nabla}_{ijk}(\rho(\theta))=0

\forall\rho\in\mathcal{M}
and
\Gamma^{\nabla^{\dagger}}_{ijk}(\rho(\eta))=0

\forall\rho\in\mathcal{M}

[
83
,
221
,
222
,
240
]
. Conversely
[
7
]
, if there exists a convex function
\Psi
such that its hessian (matrix of second derivatives) determines pointwise a riemannian metric, then there exists a pair of coordinate systems
\{\theta^{i}\}
and
\{\eta^{i}\}
and a convex function
\Psi^{\mathbf{L}}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying the above properties. The dual flatness of a pair
(\theta,\eta)
of coordinate systems is equivalent to the orthogonality of their tangent vectors at
q
with respect to the riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}
at
q
,

\mathbf{g}_{q}\left((\mathbf{T}_{q}\theta)^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{i}}\right),(\mathbf{T}_{q}\eta)^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta_{j}}\right)\right)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{i}^{j}\;\;\;\forall q\in\mathcal{M}.

(110)

The transition between these two formulations in the real finite dimensional case is provided by means of bijective
Legendre transformation

\mathbf{L}_{\Psi}:\Theta\rightarrow\Xi
, which acts between suitable open subsets
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\Xi\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and is given by the gradient,

\mathbf{L}_{\Psi}:\Theta\ni\theta\mapsto\eta:=\mathrm{grad}\Psi(\theta)\in\Xi.

(111)

In the coordinate-dependent form this reads

\displaystyle\eta_{i}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{L}_{\Psi}(\theta))_{i}:=\frac{\partial\Psi(\theta)}{\partial\theta^{i}},

(112)

\displaystyle\theta^{i}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{L}^{-1}_{\Psi}(\eta))^{i}:=\frac{\partial\Psi^{\mathbf{L}}(\eta)}{\partial\eta_{i}},

(113)

whenever the duality pairing is given by

\left[\!\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]\!\right]_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni(\theta,\eta)\mapsto\theta\cdot\eta^{\top}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{i}\eta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}.

(114)

The Eguchi equations applied to the distance

D_{\Psi}(\rho,\sigma):=\Psi(\rho)+\Psi^{\mathbf{L}}(\sigma)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{i}(\rho)\eta^{i}(\sigma)

(115)

yield
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g}^{D_{\Psi}},\nabla^{\Psi},{\nabla^{\Psi}}^{\dagger})=(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
. We will call such
D_{\Psi}
a canonical Brègman distance of a dually flat geometry
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
. A riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}
on an affine manifold
(\mathcal{M},\nabla)
with flat
\nabla
is said to be
hessian
, and denoted
\mathbf{g}^{\Psi}
, iff there exists a smooth function
\Psi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
[
319
,
320
,
63
]

\mathbf{g}(u,v)=(\nabla_{u}\mathbf{d}\Psi)(v)\;\;\forall u,v\in\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}.

(116)

Such triple
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla)
will be called a
hessian geometry

[
322
]
(see also
[
216
,
359
,
91
]
). The function
\Psi
in (
116
) is the same as in the representation of
\mathbf{g}_{ij}(\rho(\theta))
above, and

\mathbf{g}(u,v)=(\nabla^{\dagger}_{u}\mathbf{d}\Psi^{\mathbf{L}})(v)\;\;\forall u,v\in\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}.

(117)

Hence, given a riemannian manifold
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
and an affine connection
\nabla
on
\mathcal{M}
the following conditions are equivalent
[
321
,
323
,
322
]
: (1)
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla)
is a hessian geometry; (2)
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla,2\bar{\nabla}-\nabla)
is a dually flat geometry.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g},\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
be a dually flat geometry, and let
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
be
\nabla^{\dagger}
-affine (i.e., there exists a coordinate system
\{\eta^{i}\}
on
\mathcal{Q}
such that
\Gamma^{\nabla^{\dagger}}_{ijk}(\rho(\eta))=0

\forall\rho\in\mathcal{Q}
) and
\nabla^{\dagger}
-convex (i.e.
\forall\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}

\exists!

\nabla^{\dagger}
-geodesics in
\mathcal{Q}
connecting them). Then there exists a unique entropic projection

\mathcal{M}\in\rho\mapsto\mathfrak{P}^{D_{\Psi}}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\rho):=\arginf_{\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}}\left\{D_{\Psi}(\sigma,\rho)\right\}\in\mathcal{Q},

(118)

and it is equal to a unique projection
\rho_{\mathcal{Q}}
of
\rho
onto
\mathcal{Q}
along a
\nabla
-geodesic that is
(\mathbf{g},\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger})
-orthogonal at
\mathcal{Q}

[
7
,
9
]
. More precisely, the projection
\rho_{\mathcal{Q}}
is defined as such element of
\mathcal{Q}
that

\mathbf{g}_{\rho_{\mathcal{Q}}}(\dot{c}^{\nabla}(t),\dot{c}^{\nabla^{\dagger}}(s))=0\;\;\forall c^{\nabla^{\dagger}},

(119)

where
c^{\nabla}(t)
is a
\nabla
-geodesic connecting
\rho
and
\rho_{\mathcal{Q}}
, while
c^{\nabla^{\dagger}}
varies over all
\nabla^{\dagger}
-geodesics intersecting
\rho_{\mathcal{Q}}
and entirely included in
\mathcal{Q}
.

Picture 1.
\nabla
-geodesic projection onto
\nabla^{\dagger}
-affine
\nabla^{\dagger}
-convex set
\mathcal{Q}
.

Hence, for dually flat geometries, projections onto
\nabla^{\dagger}
-affine
\nabla^{\dagger}
-convex sets along
\nabla
-geodesics coincide with the entropic projections of the associated Brègman relative entropies. In consequence, one can consider a local
\nabla
-geodesic flow to be an infinitesimal version of information dynamics defined by constrained relative entropy minimisation.

2.4.2
Quantum information geometries

Given a standard representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
, consider a family of distances
D_{\mathfrak{f}}:\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\times\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
defined by
[
204
,
270
]

D_{\mathfrak{f}}(\omega,\phi):=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi),\mathfrak{f}(\Delta_{\omega,\phi}){\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi)}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(120)

if
\mathrm{supp}(\omega)\leq\mathrm{supp}(\phi)
and
D_{\mathfrak{f}}(\omega,\phi):=+\infty
otherwise, where
\mathfrak{f}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is any operator convex function (i.e.
\mathfrak{f}(tx+(1-t)y)\leq t\mathfrak{f}(x)+(1-t)\mathfrak{f}(y)

\forall x,y\in\mathcal{N}^{+}
