\forall t\in[0,1]

[
217
]
) satisfying
\mathfrak{f}(0)\leq 0
and
\mathfrak{f}(1)=0
. As proved in
[
270
,
349
]
, all
D_{\mathfrak{f}}
satisfy the condition

D(\omega,\phi)\geq D(T_{\star}(\omega),T_{\star}(\phi))\forall\omega,\phi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\;\;\forall T_{\star}:\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+},

(121)

where
T_{\star}
denotes a Banach predualisation of a weakly-
\star
continuous unital completely positive map
T:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
, and
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
is arbitrary. Moreover, the equality is attained iff
T_{\star}
is an isomorphism
[
143
]
.

For

\mathfrak{f}_{\gamma}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}t-\frac{1}{\gamma(1-\gamma)}t^{\gamma}&:\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0,1\}\\
t\log t-(t-1)&:\gamma=1\\
-\log t+(t-1)&:\gamma=0,\end{array}\right.

(122)

the restriction to
\gamma=1
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 1}
gives
[
19
,
20
]

D_{1}|_{\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 1}}(\omega,\phi)=\left\langle{{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi),\log(\Delta_{\omega,\phi}){\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi)}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

(123)

which, for
\mathcal{N}\cong{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
, turns to
[
352
,
353
]

D_{1}|_{\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{+}_{1}}(\omega,\phi)=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}(\rho_{\omega}\log(\rho_{\omega})-\rho_{\omega}\log(\rho_{\phi})).

(124)

Jenčová
[

,
185
]
proposed to consider a (Young) function

\Upsilon_{\phi}:\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\ni h\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{\phi^{h}}(\mathbb{I})+\widetilde{\phi^{-h}}(\mathbb{I}))-1\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

(125)

where
\mathcal{N}
is an arbitrary W
∗
-algebra, and

\widetilde{\phi^{h}}(\mathbb{I}):=\sup_{\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}}\{-D_{1}|_{\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 1}}(\omega,\phi)+\omega(h)+\omega(\mathbb{I})\}

(126)

With this function, she defined a noncommutative Orlicz
24
24

24

More precisely, it is a noncommutative analogue of a Morse–Transue–Krasnosel’skiĭ–Rutickiĭ space, see
[
210
]
for details.
space
L_{\Upsilon_{\phi}}(\mathcal{N})
as a completion of
\{x\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\mid\exists\lambda>0\;\;\Upsilon_{\phi}(\lambda x)<\infty\}
in the norm
{\left|\!\left|{x}\right|\!\right|}_{\Upsilon_{\phi}}:=\inf\{\lambda>0\mid\Upsilon_{\phi}(\lambda^{-1}x)\leq 1\}
. This space satisfies

\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}\sqsubseteq L_{\Upsilon_{\phi}}(\mathcal{N}):=\overline{\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}}^{{\left|\!\left|{\cdot}\right|\!\right|}_{\Upsilon_{\phi}}}.

(127)

Given the choice of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with
\dim\mathcal{H}=:n<\infty
and a smooth bijective parametrisation
\Theta\ni\theta\mapsto\rho(\theta)\in\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{+}_{0}
by the elements of an open set
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the
parametric quantum manifold
is defined as a quantum model

\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})=\left\{\rho(\theta)\in\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{+}_{0}\mid\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\right\}\subseteq\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{+}_{0}\cong\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{+}_{0}.

(128)

Usually, the additional condition
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}(\rho(\theta))=1
is imposed on the elements of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})
. A tangent space
\mathbf{T}_{\rho}\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{+}_{0}
is the real vector space of all Fréchet derivatives in the directions of smooth curves in
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{+}_{0}
that pass through
\rho
, so it can be identified with a restriction of
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{sa}}
. A restriction of
\rho
to
\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{+}_{01}
implies a restriction of the tangent vectors to the space
\{x\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{sa}}\mid\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(x)=0\}
. A Banach smooth manifold structure on
\mathcal{N}_{\star 01}^{+}
for an arbitrary countably finite
25
25

25

The W
∗
-algebras
\mathcal{N}
which are not countably finite do not allow faithful quantum states:
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}=\varnothing

W
∗

\mathcal{N}

(129)

This structure is introduced by means of the smooth atlas
\{(w_{\phi}^{-1}(U(\phi)),w_{\phi})\mid\phi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 01}\}
, where

and

w_{\phi}^{-1}:L^{0}_{\Upsilon_{\phi}}(\mathcal{N})\supseteq U(\phi)\ni h\mapsto\phi^{h}\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 01}

26

A closely related approach to construction of smooth information manifold, utilising Orlicz spaces of unbounded operators/functions instead of the MTKR spaces of bounded elements, was developed in
[

for

We conjecture that (analogously to the extension of this smooth manifold structure from

with
L_{\Upsilon_{\phi}}(\mathcal{N})
.

Given any countably finite W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
, a finite dimensional quantum model
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
that is a Banach smooth submanifold of
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, and a quantum distance
D
on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
that is smooth and satisfies (

), one can derive the corresponding quantum Norden–Sen geometry
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbf{g}^{D},\nabla^{D},{\nabla^{D}}^{\dagger})
. In particular, given any distance
D_{\mathfrak{f}}
, for
\mathcal{N}={\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{H})=\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H})^{+}_{01}
, the corresponding riemannian metric

takes the form
[

,

,

\mathbf{g}^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}}_{\rho}(u,v)=\left\langle{u,\left(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{f}}(\mathfrak{L}_{\rho}\mathfrak{R}_{\rho}^{-1})\mathfrak{R}_{\rho}\right)^{-1}(v)}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{G}_{2}(\mathcal{H})},

(131)

where
u,v\in\{x\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}\mid\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}(x)=0\}
, while
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{f}}:[0,\infty[\,\rightarrow[0,\infty[
is an operator monotone increasing function
27
27

27

A function
\mathfrak{h}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called operator monotone increasing iff
0\leq x\leq y

\Rightarrow

\mathfrak{h}(x)\leq\mathfrak{h}(y)

\forall x,y\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}

[
231
]
.
defined by

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{f}}(\lambda):=\frac{(\lambda-1)^{2}}{\mathfrak{f}(\lambda)-\lambda\mathfrak{f}(\frac{1}{\lambda})}.

(132)

This implies that several different
\mathfrak{f}
lead to the same
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{f}}
. Hence, for any riemannian metric given by (
131
) there is a family of distances
D_{\mathfrak{f}}
that have it as its second order Taylor term.

Using an integral representation

\mathfrak{f}(\lambda)=c_{1}(\lambda-1)+c_{2}(\lambda-1)^{2}+c_{3}\frac{(\lambda-1)^{2}}{\lambda}+\int_{0}^{\infty}{\tilde{\mu}}(t)(\lambda-1)^{2}\frac{1+t}{\lambda+t},

(133)

where
c_{2},c_{3}\geq 0
,
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
{\tilde{\mu}}:\,]0,\infty[\,\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
is a measure satisfying
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\tilde{\mu}}(t)\in\mathbb{R}

[
227
]
, Jenčová
[
182
]
showed that the
\mathfrak{f}
-connections
, defined by the Eguchi equation (

) applied to

distance, have the form

\mathbf{g}^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}}_{\rho}(\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}}_{x}y,z)=2\int_{0}^{\infty}{\tilde{\mu}}(\lambda)\mathrm{re}\,(\widetilde{C}(\lambda,z,x,y))-2\int_{0}^{\infty}{\tilde{\mu}}(\lambda^{-1})\left(\mathrm{re}\,(\widetilde{C}(\lambda,y,x,z)+\mathrm{re}\,(\widetilde{C}(\lambda,y,x,z))\right),

(134)

where

\widetilde{C}(\lambda,x,y,z):=(1+\lambda)\mathrm{tr}\left(x\frac{1}{\lambda\mathfrak{R}_{\rho}+\mathfrak{L}_{\rho}}(y)\frac{1}{\mathfrak{R}_{\rho}+\lambda\mathfrak{L}_{\rho}}(z)\right).

(135)

The connections
\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}}
are torsion-free. Moreover, the family of quantum Norden–Sen smooth geometries
(\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{+}_{0},\mathbf{g}^{D_{\gamma}},\nabla^{D_{\gamma}},(\nabla^{D_{\gamma}})^{\dagger})
for
\gamma\in[-1,2]
is characterised as the dually flat Eguchi geometry arising from the

distances
[

,
182
]
. This result corresponds to the class
D_{\mathfrak{f}_{\gamma}}
of quantum distances determined by (

D_{\Psi}
and
D_{\mathfrak{f}}

[
183
,
210
]
. The relationships between various information geometric objects on quantum state spaces
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
can be summarised in the following diagram:

2.4.3
Orlicz spaces and Brègman projections

As a consequence of the above results, if
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
is a dually flat manifold with respect to the triple
(\mathbf{g}^{D_{\gamma}},\nabla^{D_{\gamma}},(\nabla^{D_{\gamma}})^{\dagger})
, then
D_{\gamma}
-entropic projections onto
{(\nabla^{D_{\gamma}})^{\dagger}}
-affine-and-convex subsets are
locally equivalent
to
\nabla^{D_{\gamma}}
-geodesic “free fall”. The construction of families of
\nabla^{D_{\gamma}}
-connections in infinite dimensional noncommutative case was provided in
[
113
,
122
,
338
,
183
,
184
]
using the linear structure of noncommutative
L_{1/\gamma}(\mathcal{N})
spaces, and in such case this statement also holds
[
183
]
.

More generally (going a bit beyond the scope of the current paper), the quantum Brègman distance
D_{\Psi}
is defined via nonlinear embeddings
(\ell_{L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})(\phi)},\ell_{(L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N}))^{\star}(\phi)})
into noncommutative Orlicz spaces
L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})
and
(L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N}))^{\star}
, respectively. These spaces play the role of a tangent space ‘of states’ and the cotangent space ‘of effects’, respectively (and similarly to the commutative case of
[
115
,
113
]
). The
{\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}}^{\dagger}
-affinity and
{\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}}^{\dagger}
-convexity are defined as linear affinity and linear convexity in the Orlicz space
(L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N}))^{\star}
of effects. Thus, the global flatness of the connection on
L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})
understood as a tangent space
\mathbf{T}_{\phi}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
corresponds to its parallel transport being given by a family of isomorphisms
U_{\phi,\omega}:\mathbf{T}_{\phi}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathbf{T}_{\omega}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
satisfying
U_{\phi,\phi}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{T}_{\phi}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})}
and
U_{\phi,\omega}U_{\omega,\psi}=U_{\phi,\psi}

[
183
]
. From this point of view, the standard unitary transitions
V_{\phi,\omega}:\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
can be understood as parallel transports of the connection defined naturally by the linear structure of the fibers in the GNS Hilbert bundle. This allows us to understand
V_{\phi,\omega}
as a “free fall” along the geodesics of the Levi-Civita connection of
\mathbf{g}^{\gamma}
for
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
, known as the Wigner–Yanase metric
[
377
]
. The riemannian distance of
\mathbf{g}^{1/2}
for
\mathcal{N}\cong{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
reads
[
114
]

d_{\mathbf{g}^{1/2}}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=2\arccos\left(\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}}(\sqrt{\rho_{1}}\sqrt{\rho_{2}})\right).

(136)

On the boundary of pure spaces
\mathbf{g}^{1/2}
reduces to the Fubini–Study metric
\mathbf{g}^{\mathrm{FS}}
(
269
) multiplied by the scalar factor
4

[
272
]
, hence (
136
) divided by
2
reduces to (
271
). A generalisation of
\mathbf{g}^{1/2}
to countably additive W
∗
-algebras was provided by Connes and Størmer
[
74
]
. For a given standard representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi,J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
, it reads

\mathbf{g}^{1/2}_{\phi}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}{\left|\!\left|{(J\pi(x^{*})J-\pi(y)){\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi)}\right|\!\right|}_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

(137)

For any
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, the GNS construction equipped with the Tomita–Takesaki theory defines a corresponding standard representation
(\mathcal{H}_{\phi},\pi_{\phi},J_{\phi},\mathcal{H}^{\natural}_{\phi})
. In such case, a direct calculation based on the properties (
78
)-(
80
), using
V_{\phi,\omega}
in the role of
\mathbf{t}^{\nabla^{1/2}}_{\phi,\omega}
applied with respect to (
137
), shows that these objects satisfy the Levi-Civita version of the equation (
103
). The corresponding relative entropy is
[
183
]

D_{1/2}(\phi,\psi)=2{\left|\!\left|{u_{\phi}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\phi)-u_{\psi}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\pi}(\psi)}\right|\!\right|}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}},

(138)

where
u_{\phi}
and
u_{\psi}
are unique unitary operators arising from the polar decomposition of relative modular operators
\Delta_{\phi,\omega}
and
\Delta_{\psi,\omega}
, respectively, where
\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
is arbitrary. When expressed as a Brègman distance on the standard representation Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, this relative entropy takes the form
D_{1/2}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}{\left|\!\left|{x-y}\right|\!\right|}^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

[
183
]
. Hence, the local (infinitesimal) action of the operators
V_{\phi,\omega}
can be understood as a geodesic free fall that is locally equivalent to the minimisation of the Hilbert space norm, which in turn corresponds to a continuous linear projection operator onto a convex closed subset. In what follows, we will use the GNS Hilbert bundle having in mind the above observations.

In face of presence of other approaches to construction of smooth manifold structure on the space
L_{1}(\mathcal{X},\mho(\mathcal{X}),{\tilde{\mu}})^{+}_{01}

[
48
,
22
,
248
]
, one may ask for the specific motivation of the Orlicz space based approach. The main reason is to guarantee that the local neighbourhoods of an information state (which are identified with the tangent space) are accessible by means of entropic projection. More precisely, each tangent vector is identified as an equivalence class of one dimensional exponential models (i.e.,
p\exp(\lambda f-\log Z(p,\lambda f))
in the neighbourhood of
p\in L_{1}(\mathcal{X},\mho(\mathcal{X}),{\tilde{\mu}})^{+}_{01}
and
\phi^{\lambda h}
in the neighbourhood of
\phi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 01}
). This can be viewed as a localised version of Jaynes’ maximum entropy principle
[
166
,
172
]
(cf.
[
343
]
) of model construction. We consider it as a step that is conceptually similar to localisation of Minkowski space in the passage from special to general relativity theory: instead of working with information models that are globally exponential, we assume only that they are locally (infinitesimally) exponential. This leads to a question whether one can postulate local approximation by means of some other models (corresponding to minimisation of some different information distance functional), and construct smooth information manifold structure out of this postulate. In the commutative case this question has been answered in the affirmative by the recent work
[
357
,
356
,
358
]
, who have generalised the construction of
[
275
]
to a large family of Orlicz (more precisely, Musielak–Orlicz) spaces. The Young functions that define these spaces define the corresponding Brègman distances. We conjecture that the similar construction can be carried out in the noncommutative case. In the case when the information distance used for the construction of the smooth information manifold belongs to the Brègman class, the resulting information manifold is no longer locally exponential, but it is locally dually flat (locally hessian). Because dually flat manifolds can be thought of as a generalisation of cartesian space, this construction strengthens analogy to relationship between Minkowski space-time and general lorentzian manifold. From the perspective of applications of entropic projections, one can say that the generalisation of Jenčová’s construction of a manifold structure from one based on
D_{1}
to one based on Brègman distances
D_{\Psi}
would allow for local representations of entropic
D
-projections in terms of projections along
\nabla^{D}
-geodesics. If entropic projections are regarded as a form of information dynamics, then one can say that such construction of the smooth information manifold facilitates the possibility of introducing local (infinitesimal) representation of entropic information dynamics. Turning it to a slogan:
introducing the structure of quantum information manifold based on a Young function
\Psi
and a corresponding Brègman distance
D_{\Psi}
amounts to postulating that information flows locally along
\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}
-geodesics
.

In principle, one can construct smooth information manifold structure of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
using some distance
D
, and then consider the geometric structures and information dynamics on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
using some other distance
\tilde{D}
, or even using some class of distances,
\{\tilde{D}^{i}\mid i\in I\}
. However, using the same distance on both levels allows for stronger optimality results (concerning, for example, asymptotic estimation). More specifically, the same asymptotic results (up to third order) will be obtained for any
\widetilde{D}
that locally generates a dually flat geometry that agrees with a dually flat geometry of a Brègman distance
D_{\Psi}
. Hence, the above slogan can be equipped with a user’s notice:
a local Norden–Sen geometry and information dynamics of such manifold can be described by an arbitrary distance
D
that has the same Taylor expansion, up to third order, as
D_{\Psi}
. Given an arbitrary quantum model
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
and a distance
D
on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, the pair
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),D)
can be called
dually flat localisable
quantum geometry iff
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
can be equipped with a smooth manifold structure based on some Brègman distance
D_{\Psi}
that agrees with
D
up to third order. Such quantum geometry can be considered as a proper information geometric analogue of a lorentzian manifold: while global geometry (and dynamics) of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
is described in terms of
D
, locally it is equivalent with the description in terms of
D_{\Psi}
, which is equivalent with the description in terms of a dually flat geometry. See Section
5.3
for an application of these considerations for the problem of geometric nonperturbative renormalisation in quantum nonequilibrium statistical mechanics.

Let W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
admit a trace
\tau\in\mathcal{W}_{0}(\mathcal{N})
. A closed densely defined linear operator
x:\mathrm{dom}(x)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is called
\tau
-measurable

[
312
,
247
]
iff

\exists\lambda>0\;\;\tau(P^{{\left|{x}\right|}}(]\lambda,+\infty]))<\infty.

(139)

Let
\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{N},\tau)
denote the space of all
\tau
-measurable operators affiliated with
\pi_{\tau}(\mathcal{N})
. Let
\Upsilon:[0,\infty[\,\rightarrow[0,\infty]
be an
Orlicz function
, i.e. convex, continuous, nondecreasing,
\Upsilon(0)=0
,
\lambda>0

\Rightarrow

\Upsilon(\lambda)>0
, and
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+\infty}\Upsilon(0)=+\infty
. A noncommutative Orlicz space over
\mathcal{N}
associated with
\Upsilon
is defined as
[
220
]

L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N},\tau):=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{x\in\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{N},\tau)\mid\tau(\Upsilon({\left|{x}\right|}))\leq 1\},

(140)

equipped with a norm

{\left|\!\left|{\cdot}\right|\!\right|}_{\Upsilon}:\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{N},\tau)\ni x\mapsto\inf\{\lambda>0\mid\tau(\Upsilon(\lambda^{-1}{\left|{x}\right|}))\leq 1\}

(141)

which turn it into a Banach space. It follows that

L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N},\tau)=\{x\in\mathscr{M}(\mathcal{N},\tau)\mid\exists\lambda>0\;\;\tau(\Upsilon(\lambda{\left|{x}\right|}))<\infty\}.

(142)

An issue of canonical generalisation of the notion of a noncommutative Orlicz space to an arbitrary W
∗
-algebra is a matter of a current research, see
[
212
]
for a discussion.

The construction of a general notion of a quantum Brègman distance for arbitrary spaces
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
is an open problem. Let
X
be a reflexive Banach space, let
\Psi:X\rightarrow\,]-\infty,+\infty]
be convex, lower semi-continuous, and Legendre (see
[
26
]
for a definition). Let
C\subseteq X
be nonempty and convex,
C\cap\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{efd}(\Psi))\neq\varnothing
, where
\mathrm{efd}(\Psi):=\{x\in X\mid\Psi(x)\neq+\infty\}
, and let
y\in\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{efd}(\Psi))
. Then the
Brègman functional
on
X
, defined by

\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(x,y):=\Psi(x)-\Psi(y)-\left[\!\left[x-y,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{G}}_{y}\Psi(y)\right]\!\right]_{X\times X^{\star}}

(143)

for
y\in\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{efd}(\Psi))
, and
\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(x,y)=+\infty
otherwise, satisfies
[
26
]
:

1)

\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(\cdot,y)
is convex and lower semi-continuous,

2)

\mathrm{efd}(\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(\cdot,y))=\mathrm{efd}(\Psi)
,

3)

\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(x,y)=0

\iff

x=y
,

4)

\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}}_{C}(y)=\{*\}\in\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{efd}(\Psi))
,

5)

\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}}_{C}\circ\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}}_{C}(y)=\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}}_{C}(y)
,

6)

if
K
is a vector subspace of
X
, then Chencov’s generalised pythagorean theorem
[
57
,
59
]
holds:

\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(x,y)=\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(x,\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}}_{K}(y))+\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}}_{K}(y),y)\;\;\forall(x,y)\in K\times X.

(144)

Let
X\cong L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})
, and consider a map
\ell_{L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})}:\mathcal{N}_{\star}\rightarrow L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})
satisfying
\ell_{L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+})\subseteq(L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N}))^{+}
,
\ell_{L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})}(\mathcal{N}_{\star})\subseteq\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{efd}(\Psi))
, and bijective on its codomain. Then the
quantum Brègman distance
is defined as
[
213
]

D_{\Psi}(\phi,\psi):=\widetilde{D}_{\Psi}(\ell_{L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})}(\phi),\ell_{L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})}(\psi))\;\;\forall(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}\times\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}.

(145)

The nontrivial open problem consists of finding the minimal additional conditions on
\Psi
and
\ell_{L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})}
that are necessary and sufficient to prove that
D_{\Psi}(\phi,\psi)
is smooth (or at least triple Gâteaux differentiable) and satisfies the infinite dimensional analogue of the property (
103
) as well as an equivalence of
D_{\Psi}
-projections onto linear convex closed subspaces
\mathcal{Q}
of
L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})
with
(\mathbf{g}^{D_{\Psi}},\nabla^{D_{\Psi}},(\nabla^{D_{\Psi}})^{\dagger})
-orthogonal projections along
\nabla^{D_{\Psi}}
-geodesics onto
\mathcal{Q}
. These conditions will necessarily intertwine the properties of
\Psi
,
\Upsilon
, and
\ell_{L_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{N})}
. See
[
213
]
for an additional discussion.

2.4.4
Conjecture: a Morozova–Chencov–Petz bundle

Let
(\mathcal{H}_{\phi},\pi_{\phi},\Omega_{\phi})
be a GNS representation of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
for
\phi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
. Consider a scalar product on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, defined by

\displaystyle\left\langle{[x]_{\phi},[y]_{\phi}}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle{[x^{*}]_{\phi},\mathfrak{J}^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\phi,\phi}([y]_{\phi})}\right\rangle_{\phi},

(146)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{J}^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\phi,\psi}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{h}(\Delta_{\phi,\psi})}\mathfrak{R}(\psi)^{-1},

(147)

where
\mathfrak{R}(\psi)
is a right multiplication by
\psi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}

[
318
]
, while
\mathfrak{h}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
is an operator monotone increasing function satisfying
[
271
]

\mathfrak{h}(\lambda)=\lambda\mathfrak{h}(\lambda^{-1})

\forall\lambda>0
(all functions
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{f}}
given by (
132
) satisfy this property). We conjecture that:

1)

the completion of the vector space
\pi_{\phi}(\mathcal{N})/\ker(\mathfrak{J}^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\phi})
in the scalar product (
146
) is a Hilbert space (denoted below as
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}
, with elements denoted by
[x]_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}
for any
x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\phi}
),

2)

\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}(x):[y]_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}\mapsto[xy]_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}
defines a nondegenerate faithful normal representation
\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{h},\phi})
,

3)

a unique extension of the antilinear isometry
J_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}:[x]_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}\mapsto[x^{*}]_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}

\forall x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\phi}
determines a conjugation on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}
, turning a quadruple

\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{h},\phi},\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\phi},J_{\mathfrak{h},\phi},\bigcup_{x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\phi}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{\phi}^{*}}\overline{\left\{\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}(x)J_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}[x]_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}\right\}}^{\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{h},\phi}}\right)

(148)

to a standard representation.

If this conjecture holds, then the above construction determines a bundle of Hilbert spaces over any topological space
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
. We will refer to it as a Morozova–Chencov–Petz bundle (it could be defined equivalently using the Morozova–Chencov functions
c
instead of Petz’s functions
\mathfrak{h}
, see e.g.
[
213
]
). Clearly, it provides an alternative to the GNS bundle. A virtue of the MCP bundle is that it encodes the local riemannian geometry of the state space in the variability of changes of the scalar product. Yet, it remains an open problem whether the above conjecture is true.

2.5
Local gauge and geodesic propagation

If
\mathfrak{g}
is a Banach Lie algebra with a Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
, then a
representation
of
\mathfrak{g}
on a dense subset
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is defined as a linear function
\mathfrak{a}
mapping each
x\in\mathfrak{g}
to an anti-selfadjoint operator
\mathfrak{a}(x):\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
such that

\mathfrak{a}([x,y])=\mathfrak{a}(x)\mathfrak{a}(y)-\mathfrak{a}(y)\mathfrak{a}(x)\;\;\forall x,y\in\mathfrak{g}.

(149)

Hence, for a given representation
\mathfrak{a}
of
\mathfrak{g}
on
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, every
x\in\mathfrak{g}
determines a unique self-adjoint, and generally unbounded, operator
\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}(x)
. By definition,
\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{dom}(\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}(x))
.

Let
G
be a Lie group, and let
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star}
be equipped with a principal
G
-bundle
E\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, and a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued connection one-form
\mathbf{A}
on
E
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra of
G
. Moreover, assume that the GNS bundle
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
is equipped with the family
\mathfrak{a}
of the representations of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
,
28
28

28

This definition covers also the representations of
\mathfrak{g}
in the well-adapted Banach–Lie subalgebras
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathfrak{e}^{-1}(\omega)
(thus, within the fibers of
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
) as the special case.

\mathfrak{a}:=\{\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow(\mathrm{Lin}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega}))^{\mathrm{asa}}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\}.

(150)

The triple
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a})
satisfying the above conditions will be called a
local gauge model
, while the pair
(\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a})
will be called a
local gauge structure
on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
.
29
29

29

The term
gauge
means the section of a principal
G
-bundle
[
369
,
370
]
. The
local gauge
means the local section, while the
global gauge
means the global section. A particularly interesting example of a local gauge structure is provided by the choice of a locally compact and connected Lie group
G=\mathrm{SO}^{\uparrow}(1,3)\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{4}
, known as
ortochronous Poincaré group
.
In principle, a given manifold
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
can admit various different local gauge structures.

If the model
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
is equipped with the local gauge structure, then any curve
c:\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\phi(t)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
corresponds also to a specific choice of a section of the principal
G
-bundle
E
along this trajectory, which can be expressed by means of integral of a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued connection 1-form
\mathbf{A}
.

If
\mathfrak{a}
is determined by setting
\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathfrak{g})
to be equal to the generators of the irreducible unitary representation of an action of
G
on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
, then one can apply Wigner’s theorem
[
376
]
to each fibre of
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})

separately
, classifying the elements of
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
into subsets by means of their transformation properties. According to Wigner’s interpretation of this mathematical property (which became widely accepted afterwards), an element of
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
transforming under the above representation of
G
shall be understood as a pure state of a ‘quantum particle’, where pure state means a vector in a Hilbert space.

Our framework allows to enrich this interpretation by considering a propagation of a ‘quantum particle’ state over the trajectory on the manifold
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, using the
\mathfrak{g}
-valued connection one-form
\mathbf{A}
. As discussed in Section

2.2
, if
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, then every two standard representations determine a unique standard unitary transition between them that preserves the standard cone. Hence, one can map uniquely between the elements of the fibres
\mathcal{H}_{\phi_{1}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\phi_{2}}
, whenever
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, by means of the standard unitary transition operator
V_{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}
. Let
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
, let
c:[0,t]\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
be a curve with
c(0)=\omega
and
c(t)=\phi
, let
\xi\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
. Then
\zeta
will be called an
\mathbf{A}
-propagation
of
\xi
along
c
iff
\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\phi}(\int_{c}\mathbf{A})-\mathrm{i}J_{\phi}\mathfrak{a}_{\phi}(\int_{c}\mathbf{A})J_{\phi}
is essentially self-adjoint on
\mathrm{dom}(\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\phi}(\int_{c}\mathbf{A}))\cap\mathrm{dom}(\mathrm{i}J_{\phi}\mathfrak{a}_{\phi}(\int_{c}\mathbf{A})J_{\phi})
and

\zeta=U^{\mathbf{A}}_{c,\phi}(t)V_{\phi,\omega}\xi:=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\phi}(\int_{c(t)}\mathbf{A})+\mathrm{i}J_{\phi}\mathfrak{a}_{\phi}(\int_{c(t)}\mathbf{A})J_{\phi})}V_{\phi,\omega}\xi=\mathrm{e}^{t([(\mathfrak{a}_{\phi}(\int_{c(t)}\mathbf{A})),\,\cdot\,])}V_{\phi,\omega}\xi.

(151)

The operator
V_{\phi,\omega}
is a parallel transport associated with the natural connection in the GNS Hilbert bundle determined by the linear structure of the Hilbert space. Hence, the equation (
151
) can be understood as an updating map
\xi\mapsto\zeta
along the trajectory
c(t)
that takes into account both
\mathbf{A}
and
\nabla^{\mathbf{g}^{1/2}}
connections.

The above construction suggests introducing more tight relationship between the connection structures of
\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
for
any
local gauge model
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a})
such that
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
can be equipped with a smooth manifold structure, and with
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
. Two quite intriguing possibilities are:

(QP
1
)

introduce an affine connection
\nabla
on
\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
and define
gauge geodesic propagation
as an
\mathbf{A}
-propagation along
\nabla
-geodesic in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
;

(QP
2
)

introduce: i) an action of
G
on
\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
turning it to a tangent
G
-bundle, ii) a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued connection 1-form
\mathbf{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbf{T}}
on
\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, and iii) a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued connection 1-form
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
on
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, such that the
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
-parallel transports along
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
-geodesics in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
are equal to
U^{\mathbf{A}}_{c,\phi}(t)V_{\phi,\omega}
or
V_{\phi,\omega}U^{\mathbf{A}}_{c,\omega}(t)
(where
t
is an affine parameter of an
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
-geodesic
c(t)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
), and define
gauge geodesic propagation
as a horizontal lift of an
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
-geodesic in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
with respect to
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
. These gauge geodesic propagations are precisely the
\mathbf{A}
-propagations along
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
-geodesics in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
. So, this what we gain by such definition is an additional structure on
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
that allows for further study of a relationship between
(G,\mathfrak{g},\mathbf{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\cdot})
-structures of
\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
. On the other hand, the price paid is the requirement that the
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
-parallel transport along
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
-geodesic depends only on its endpoint, which holds if
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
is simply connected and
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
is flat.

If the definition (QP
1
) is used, and
G
and
\mathfrak{a}
are chosen as for Wigner’s ‘quantum particle’ classification discussed above, then the gauge geodesic propagation has a direct interpretation as an
\mathbf{A}
-propagation of a ‘quantum particle’ due to “free fall” along
\nabla
-geodesic. Note that (despite ‘dynamical’ feeling associated with the word ‘fall’) this propagation has no ‘dynamical’ (causal) content: it is an extension of the description of gauge transformation properties of a ‘quantum particle’ state from a single Hilbert space to a Hilbert space fibre bundle over a quantum model equipped with a local gauge structure. In such approach, a ‘quantum particle’ becomes identified with a (not necessarily global) section of a fibre
G
-bundle represented in terms of the fibre bundle of the GNS Hilbert spaces, so one can discuss its quantum propagation between some ‘source model’ and some ‘sink model’, defined as suitable submanifolds of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
. Under the choice of a definition (QP
1
), we will define a
gauge geodesic propagation model
as a local gauge model
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a})
equipped with an affine connection
\nabla
.

The motivation and interpretation of these definitions echoes Einstein’s postulate
[
98
,
97
]
of identification of geodesic lines with the «world-lines of freely moving point-particles», but generalised from the pseudo-riemannian geometry, for which the geodesics of the Levi-Civita connection coincide with the curves of extreme distance of a pseudo-riemannian metric, to the setting of general affine connections
[
307
]
(see e.g.
[
294
,
234
]
and references therein for a comparative discussion of these two approaches). The above construction of geodesic propagation of ‘quantum particles’ is partially influenced by the works of Drechsler
[
87
,
88
,
89
]
and Prugovečki
[
278
,
279
,
280
,
281
,
282
,
283
,
285
,
284
]
(see also
[
123
,
124
]
). As opposed to them, we do not require any pseudo-riemannian metric on the base manifold, so we do not introduce soldered Poincaré frame bundles, and we also consider the GNS Hilbert spaces (which may be unitarily inequivalent, if
\omega\notin\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
) varying over the base manifold instead of pasting fibre bundle from identical copies of a single Hilbert space. Moreover, our manifold is a space of quantum states over W
∗
-algebras, as opposed to
a priori
postulated background space-time. On the other hand, similarly to Prugovečki (see
[
281
,
283
,
284
]
), and as opposed to the approaches of Wightman
[
371
,
372
,
373
,
374
]
and Haag–Kastler
[
126
,
128
]
, we impose the requirement of Poincaré covariance not on the
topological
subsets of the base manifold and on the (presheaves of) algebras of operators associated (functorially
[
47
]
) to those subsets, but on the fibres of
geometric
fibre bundle and on the fibre bundle of Hilbert spaces over this manifold. (Note that, in addition, one can also introduce an independent group covariance requirement on the elements of an underlying W
∗
-algebra, determining this way their transformation properties at each fibre by means of the GNS representation.)

If
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
is equipped with the structure of quantum information manifold, and with a quantum information distance
D_{\mathfrak{f}}
, then (at least in the finite dimensional case)
\nabla
can be chosen to be given by
\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}}
,
(\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}})^{\dagger}
, or
(\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}}+(\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}})^{\dagger})/2
(the latter is a Levi–Civita of the riemannian geometry
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbf{g}^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}})
). Moreover, if
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
is flat with respect to
\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}}
and
(\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}})^{\dagger}
, then one can identify the entropic projection of the associated Brègman distance
D_{\Psi}
with the geodesic projection by
\nabla^{D_{\mathfrak{f}}}
. If
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{f}_{\gamma}
, then
D_{\mathfrak{f}}
is also a Brègman distance. In such case, an assumption (QP
1
) and identification of
\nabla
with
\nabla^{D_{\gamma}}
, allows to state that the propagation of a ‘quantum particles’ is due to the geodesic “free fall” along the (local) information dynamics, determined by a constrained minimisation of a relative entropy.

3
Locally perturbed liouvilleans

In this Section we will use the approach to unbounded ‘perturbations’ of liouvilleans, developed by the Dereziński, Jakšić and Pillet in
[
80
]
, and based on earlier results of Araki
[
17
,
16
]
. According to
[
80
]
, if

1.

\mathcal{C}\subseteq{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}
is a von Neumann algebra,

2.

(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R},\varsigma)
is a W
∗
-dynamical system with a standard liouvillean
K^{\varsigma}
associated with a standard form
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{C},J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
of
\mathcal{C}
,

3.

Q
is a self-adjoint linear operator affiliated
to
\mathcal{C}
,

4.

K^{\varsigma}+Q
is an essentially self-adjoint linear operator on
\mathrm{dom}(K^{\varsigma})\cap\mathrm{dom}(Q)\subseteq\mathcal{H}
,

then for

\varsigma_{t}^{Q}(x):=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(K^{\varsigma}+Q)}x\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(K^{\varsigma}+Q)}\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{C}\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}

(152)

the following statements are true:

1.

(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{R},\varsigma^{Q})
is a W
∗
-dynamical system.

2.

the operator

E_{\varsigma,Q}(t):=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(K^{\varsigma}+Q)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tK^{\varsigma}}\in\mathcal{C},

(153)

called an
expansional
, is unitary and for all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and all
x\in\mathcal{C}
it satisfies the following cocycle conditions:

\displaystyle\varsigma_{t}^{Q}(x)

\displaystyle=E_{\varsigma,Q}(t)\varsigma_{t}(x)E_{\varsigma,Q}(t)^{-1},

(154)

\displaystyle E_{\varsigma,Q}(t)^{-1}

\displaystyle=E_{\varsigma,Q}(t)^{*}=\varsigma_{t}(E_{\varsigma,Q}(-t)),

(155)

\displaystyle E_{\varsigma,Q}(t_{1}+t_{2})

\displaystyle=E_{\varsigma,Q}(t_{1})\varsigma_{t_{1}}(E_{\varsigma,Q}(t_{2})).

(156)

3.

if

K^{\varsigma,Q}:=K^{\varsigma}+Q-JQJ

(157)

is an essentially self-adjoint linear operator on
\mathrm{dom}(K^{\varsigma})\cap\mathrm{dom}(Q)\cap\mathrm{dom}(JQJ)
, then a unique self-adjoint extension of
K^{\varsigma,Q}
, denoted (with an abuse of notation) by the same symbol, is a standard liouvillean of
\varsigma^{Q}
in
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{C},J,\mathcal{H}^{\natural})
. We will call
K^{\varsigma,Q}
and
\varsigma^{Q}
a
Dereziński--Jakšić--Pillet perturbation
of
K^{\varsigma}
and
\varsigma
, respectively.

4.

if
Q
is bounded, then, for any
x\in\mathcal{C}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the Dyson–Feynman–Fujiwara–Araki perturbative expansions
[
92
,
101
,
105
,
15
]
,

\displaystyle\varsigma_{t}^{Q}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{i}^{n}\int\limits_{0\leq t_{n}\leq\cdots\leq t_{1}\leq t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}[\varsigma_{t_{n}}(Q),[\ldots,{[}\varsigma_{t_{1}}(Q),\varsigma_{t}(x)]\ldots]],

(158)

\displaystyle E_{\varsigma,Q}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{i}^{n}\int\limits_{0\leq t_{n}\leq\cdots\leq t_{1}\leq t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}\varsigma_{t_{n}}(Q)\cdots\varsigma_{t_{1}}(Q).

(159)

are convergent in weak-
\star
topology and define a norm convergent series of bounded operators. Moreover, the associated generators
\eth
of
\varsigma
and
\eth_{Q}
of
\varsigma^{Q}
have in such case the same domain, and are related by

\eth_{\varsigma^{Q}}(x)=\eth_{\varsigma}(x)+\mathrm{i}[Q,x]\;\;\forall x\in\mathrm{dom}(\eth_{\varsigma}).

(160)

3.1
Local gauge liouvilleans

In Section
2.3
we have observed that the Poisson flow of an algebraic hamiltonian vector field on
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
can be always represented by a unitary evolution in a fibre of the GNS bundle
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{N}_{\star 0}^{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, generated by a standard liouvillean operator on this fibre. In what follows, we will abstract this relationship, replacing tangent bundle by a principal
G
-bundle and replacing the procedure of
restriction
of a
global
algebraic evolution, by the procedure of
extension
of a
local
algebraic evolution in order to incorporate geometric structure as an additional component of an effective dynamics.

If one
assumes
that the principal
G
-bundle structure participates in an effective form of a temporal evolution, then this evolution shall be represented not by the standard liouvillean on
\mathcal{H}_{\phi(t)}
alone, but by the standard liouvillean perturbed by the ‘gauge connection’ operator
\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\phi(t)}(\mathbf{A})
, which represents the change of vectors in the fibres
\mathcal{H}_{\phi(t)}
caused by the fibrewise action of the group
G
and the choice of an
\mathbf{A}
-section of a principal
G
-bundle
E
. (When the trajectory along the curve
c:[0,t]\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
with
c(0)=\phi(0)
and
c(t)=\phi(t)
is investigated as a source of memory effects, then
\mathbf{A}
should be replaced by
\int_{\phi(0)}^{\phi(t)}\mathbf{A}
.)

For this purpose, we will use DJP perturbation approach, setting
\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
,
\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
,
\mathcal{C}:=\pi_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})
,
Q:=\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathbf{A})
. We can define
\varsigma
in two different ways. If
\mathcal{N}
is equipped with a W
∗
-dynamical system structure
(\mathcal{N},\mathbb{R},\alpha)
, then one can define
\varsigma
globally in each fiber of the GNS bundle by means of

\varsigma_{t}(\pi_{\omega}(x)):=\pi_{\omega}(\alpha_{t}(x))\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}\;\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\;\;\forall\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}).

(161)

Alternatively, if
\mathcal{N}
is equipped with a quantum Poisson system
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}),h)
such that
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}h\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}

\forall\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, then one can define
\varsigma
pointwisely in each fiber by means of

\varsigma_{t}(\pi_{\omega}(x)):=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}h)}\pi_{\omega}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}h)}\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}\;\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\;\;\forall\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}).

(162)

We will call these assuptions a
generalised Poisson compatibility condition
(PC
2
). If
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}
,
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}})
is a submanifold of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}_{\star}
, and the pair
(h,\alpha)
satisfies (
97
), then the above two definitions of
\varsigma
agree. The difference between (
162
) and (
161
) (the latter corresponding to the covariance equation (
85
)) indicates our approach to quantum dynamics, as being defined locally by the differential geometric properties of state space, instead of a global automorphism of an underlying algebraic structure. Alternatively, if
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
is equipped with a quantum information manifold structure and a global vector field
\mathfrak{X}_{h}\in\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
such that
\mathfrak{X}_{h}(\phi)\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}

\forall\phi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, then
\varsigma
can be defined by

\varsigma_{t}(\pi_{\omega}(x)):=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{X}_{h}(\omega))}\pi_{\omega}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{X}_{h}(\omega))}\;\;\forall x\in\mathcal{N}\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\;\forall\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}).

(163)

Thus, under some relatively weak conditions (affiliation of
\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathbf{A})
with
\pi_{\omega}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
and essential self-adjointness of sums
K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathbf{A})
and
K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathbf{A})-\mathrm{i}J_{\omega}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}(\mathbf{A})J_{\omega}
on the intersection of domains of their components), the local gauge structure can be incorporated in the redefinition of the standard liouvillean. If

K_{\omega}^{\varsigma,\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}(\mathbf{A})}=K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)-\mathrm{i}J_{\omega}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)J_{\omega}

(164)

satisfies the above conditions, then we will call it a
local gauge liouvillean
at
\omega
.

Let
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a})
be a local gauge model with
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
and let
\varsigma
be defined as above, either by a quantum Poisson system
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}),h)
with
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}h\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}

\forall\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, or by a W
∗
-dynamical system
(\mathcal{N},\mathbb{R},\alpha)
with
\alpha_{\star}^{t}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}))\subseteq\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})

\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\rho_{\star}
denote
w^{h}
or
\alpha_{\star}
, respectively.
If for every
\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
there exists a family of local gauge liouvilleans

K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)-\mathrm{i}J_{\omega}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)J_{\omega},

(165)

parametrised by
t\in\mathbb{R}
, then the quadruple
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\varsigma,\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a})
will be called a
local gauge liouvillean model
. From the above construction we see that the W
∗
-dynamical system
(\pi_{\omega}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbb{R},\varsigma^{\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}(\mathbf{A})})
may not correspond to any W
∗
-dynamical system on
\mathcal{N}
. The description of temporal evolution in terms of
\varsigma^{\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}(\mathbf{A})}
is ‘local’ in the sense that it is provided inside of each fibre of the GNS bundle independently.

3.2
Local source liouvilleans

In principle, apart from the ‘internal’ dynamics (implemented by the evolution
\varsigma^{t}
) and the kinematic local gauge structure, the effective dynamics can also depend on some controlled ‘external’ constraints. We will assume that these constraints can be specified in terms of external ‘sources’, which can generally be represented by the variations
\delta(\phi(x))
of expectation values. These variations can be decomposed into two parts: the variations
(\delta\phi)(x)
of states, and the variations
\phi(\delta x)
of operators. The constraints on changes
\delta\phi
can be handled by restricting the form of the model
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
(for a geometric approach, see
[
241
,
177
,
100
,
209
]
). On the other hand, note that
\delta x
can be in principle arbitrary, so it can also depend on
\phi
, and it may not arise as an infinitesimal change generated by a global automorphism of
\mathcal{N}
(thus, it cannot be described by the setting of derivations of C
∗
-algebras). We will implement the perturbations
\delta x
of elements
x
of (a local GNS representation of) a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
by means of state dependent perturbations of liouvilleans. In this sense, the constraints on changes of operators will be handled by local (state dependent) additional terms modifying liouvillean evolution.

For this purpose, apart from local gauge structure on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
, we introduce also
local source term
, defined as a fibrewise family of operators

(\lambda,H):\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\ni\omega\mapsto\lambda(\omega)H(\omega)\in(\mathrm{Lin}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega}))^{\mathrm{sa}},

(166)

with
\lambda(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}
called
local source strength
and
H(\omega)\in(\mathrm{Lin}(\mathcal{H}_{\omega}))^{\mathrm{sa}}
called
local source operator
. If
\lambda(\omega)
is independent of
\omega
, then it will be called
global source strength
. The
(2n+1)
-tuple

(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),(\lambda_{1},H_{1}),\ldots,(\lambda_{n},H_{n}))

(167)

will be called
local source model
iff
(\lambda_{i},H_{i})
is a local source term for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. If (
167
) is a local source model,
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
,
\varsigma
is defined as in Section
3.1
, and for
\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
the DJP perturbation of
K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}
by
\lambda_{1}(\omega)H_{1}(\omega)+\ldots+\lambda_{n}(\omega)H_{n}(\omega)
exists, then a unique self-adjoint extension of an essentially self-adjoint operator

K_{\omega}^{\varsigma,\lambda_{1}H_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}H_{n}}=K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}+\lambda_{1}(\omega)H_{1}(\omega)+\ldots+\lambda_{n}(\omega)H_{n}(\omega)-J_{\omega}(\lambda_{1}(\omega)H_{1}(\omega)+\ldots+\lambda_{n}(\omega)H_{n}(\omega))J_{\omega}

(168)

will be called
local source liouvillean
at
\omega
. If a local source liouvillean exists for each
\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, then the
2(n+1)
-tuple
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\varsigma,\lambda_{1},H_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},H_{n})
will be called
local source liouvillean model
.

Let us note that a local source
\lambda_{i}(\omega)H_{i}(\omega)
at
\omega
should be understood not as the
i
-th type “interaction source”
localised
at
\omega
, but as a strength-and-action of the
i
-th type “interaction source”
perceived
at location
\omega
.

Let
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a})
be local gauge model. Let
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\lambda_{1},H_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},H_{n})
be local source model. Let
\varsigma
be such as defined in Section
3.1
. If, for a given
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, there exists a DJP perturbation of a standard liouvillean
K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}
, given by the unique self-adjoint extension of an essentially self-adjoint operator

\mathcal{L}(\omega,t):=K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)-\mathrm{i}J_{\omega}\mathfrak{a}_{\omega}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)J_{\omega}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\lambda_{i}(\omega)H_{i}(\omega)-J_{\omega}\lambda_{i}(\omega)H_{i}(\omega)J_{\omega}\right),

(169)

then
\mathcal{L}(\omega,t)
will be called a
local liouvillean operator
at
(\omega,t)
. If
\mathcal{L}(\omega,t)
exists for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and all
\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, then the
(4+2n)
-tuple

(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\varsigma,\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a},\lambda_{1},H_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},H_{n})

(170)

will be called an
local liouvillean model
. In the special case, all of operators
H_{1},\ldots,H_{n}
can be determined by the elements
h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}
of a W
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
, with

H_{i}(h_{i}):\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\ni\omega\mapsto H_{i}(\omega):=\pi_{\omega}(h_{i})\in{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H})}^{\mathrm{sa}}.

(171)

This allows, in particular, for a fibrewise representation of a ‘global gauge’ action
G_{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
of some Lie group
G_{0}
(not necessarily related to
G
), whenever
\{h_{i}\}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
are the generators of the representation of
G_{0}
in
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
. This observation can be generalised to
l
subsets of
\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}\}
playing the role of generators of
l
representations of
l
Lie groups
G_{l}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{N})
.

3.3
Case study: The BLP perspective on nonlinear quantum fields

Restriction of considerations from topological spaces
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
to BLP manifolds
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
allows us to equip the operator algebraic approach with an additional differential geometric content, using Fréchet derivatives of smooth functions on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
in the role of differential forms. In this Section we will investigate the possibility of interpretation of these forms as nonlinear quantum fields (understood in quite formal sense).

If
f\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{B}_{\star};\mathbb{R})
and
\phi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
, then
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\phi}f=\mathbf{d}_{\phi}f\equiv\mathbf{d}f(\phi)\in\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\phi}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
. Thus, if
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
,
f\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B});\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}f\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}

\forall\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
, and
\varsigma
is defined as in the previous two Sections, then one can consider local source liouvilleans determined by the perturbation

K_{\omega}^{\varsigma}+\lambda(\omega)\left(\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}f)-J_{\omega}\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}f)J_{\omega}\right),

(172)

for some family
\lambda(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}

\forall\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
. As opposed to a function used for definition of
\varsigma
and
K^{\varsigma}_{\omega}
,
f
is not considered as a generator of a Poisson flow, and it is allowed to be arbitrary rescaled by
\lambda(\omega)
at each point.

Every
x\in\mathcal{B}
can be represented again as a smooth function on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
by means of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}\ni\omega\mapsto\omega(x)\in\mathbb{R}
, allowing to consider elements of
\mathcal{B}
arising from multiple Fréchet differentiation,

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega_{n}}(\phi_{n-1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega_{n-1}}(\cdots(\phi_{1}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega_{1}}f)))))\in\mathcal{B},

(173)

for
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n-1}\in\mathcal{B}
. These derivatives can be added and multiplied as elements of
\mathcal{B}
, and any of the resulting elements of
\mathcal{B}
can be subjected to a representation in the GNS bundle as a local source operator. However, despite multiple application of Fréchet differentiation, objects of type (
173
) are (just) elements of
\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\omega_{n}}\mathcal{B}_{\star}
. This leads us to ask whether it is possible to introduce higher order tensors on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, which could be used as source terms acting on the GNS bundle. The natural candidates for this purpose are
(n,m)
-tensor fields
over
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, defined pointwisely as

\mathfrak{X}_{k_{1}}(\phi)\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathfrak{X}_{k_{n}}(\phi)\boxtimes\mathbf{d}f_{1}(\phi)\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathbf{d}f_{m}(\phi),

(174)

which are the elements of

\left(\bigboxtimes^{n}\mathbf{T}_{\phi}\mathcal{B}_{\star}\right)\boxtimes\left(\bigboxtimes^{m}\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\phi}\mathcal{B}\right)\cong\left(\bigboxtimes^{n}\mathcal{B}_{\star}\right)\boxtimes\left(\bigboxtimes^{m}\mathcal{B}\right),

(175)

where
\boxtimes
denotes the tensor product considered in an algebraic sense (that is, without taking topological completion) and the dependence on
\phi
is assumed to be smooth. The contraction at
\phi
of an
(n,m-1)
-tensor field with an
(n,m)
-tensor field by means of the componentwise application of the duality
\left[\!\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]\!\right]_{\mathcal{B}_{\star}\times\mathcal{B}}
gives a one-form at
\phi
, which belongs to
\mathcal{B}
:

\displaystyle\left[\!\left[\mathbf{d}l_{1}(\phi)\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathbf{d}l_{n}(\phi)\boxtimes\mathfrak{X}_{h_{1}}(\phi)\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes(\cdot)_{i}\boxtimes\cdots\mathfrak{X}_{h_{m}}(\phi),\mathfrak{X}_{k_{1}}(\phi)\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathfrak{X}_{k_{n}}(\phi)\boxtimes\mathbf{d}f_{1}(\phi)\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathbf{d}f_{m}(\phi)\right]\!\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{X}_{k_{1}}(\phi),\mathbf{d}l_{1}(\phi)\right]\!\right]\cdots\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{X}_{k_{n}}(\phi),\mathbf{d}l_{n}(\phi)\right]\!\right]\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{X}_{h_{1}}(\phi),\mathbf{d}f_{1}(\phi)\right]\!\right]\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{X}_{h_{i-1}}(\phi),\mathbf{d}f_{i-1}(\phi)\right]\!\right]\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{X}_{h_{i+1}}(\phi),\mathbf{d}f_{i+1}(\phi)\right]\!\right]

\displaystyle\cdots\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{X}_{h_{m}}(\phi),\mathbf{d}f_{m}(\phi)\right]\!\right]\cdot\mathbf{d}f_{i}(\phi)=:\lambda(\phi)\mathbf{d}f_{i}(\phi).

(176)

When subjected to representation as a source term,
\lambda(\phi)
is a natural candidate for a local source strength of a local source operator
\pi_{\phi}(\mathbf{d}f_{i}(\phi))
. We will (tentatively) call the local source operators of this type
quantum fields
. One can also introduce the antisymmetric wedge product on vectors and covectors (one forms) and define the corresponding contraction to 1-form and its source term representation in an analogous way. In particular, for
\mathcal{B}\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, a vector field
\mathfrak{X}_{k}\in\mathbf{T}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
can be represented in terms of a GNS fibre bundle
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
as a family

\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})\ni\phi\mapsto\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}}(\phi)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi})_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\cong\mathfrak{G}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi})^{\mathrm{sa}}

(177)

determined by

\left[\!\left[\mathfrak{X}_{k}(\phi),\mathbf{d}f(\phi)\right]\!\right]_{\mathbf{T}_{\phi}\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\times\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\phi}\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}}=:\left[\!\left[\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{k}}(\phi),\pi_{\phi}(\mathbf{d}f(\phi))\right]\!\right]_{\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi})_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\times\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi})^{\mathrm{sa}}}\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\;\forall f\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}};\mathbb{R}).

(178)

The GNS representation of
(0,n)
-tensor fields with
n>1
is also possible, however it can be provided in different ways. For example, given a
(0,2)
-tensor field
(\mathbf{d}f\boxtimes\mathbf{d}k)(\phi)
, it is possible to represent it as:

\displaystyle\pi_{\phi}(\mathbf{d}f(\phi))\otimes\pi_{\phi}(\mathbf{d}k(\phi))

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi})\otimes\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi}),

(179)

\displaystyle\pi_{\phi}(\mathbf{d}f(\phi))\otimes\pi_{\phi}(\mathbf{d}k(\phi))

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\phi}),

(180)

\displaystyle\pi_{\phi\otimes\phi}((\mathbf{d}f\boxtimes\mathbf{d}k)(\phi))

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi\otimes\phi}),

(181)

where
(\mathcal{H}_{\phi\otimes\phi},\pi_{\phi\otimes\phi},\Omega_{\phi\otimes\phi})
is the GNS representation of
\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{N}
in the state
\phi\otimes\phi\in(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{N})_{\star}^{+}
. It seems that the representation (
181
) preserves most precisely the geometric content of
(0,2)
-tensor field, so we feel tempted to consider it as a preferred construction. However, this leads us to construction of a whole family of fibre bundles of Hilbert spaces over the manifold
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
. It is unclear at this stage whether this phenomenon should be considered as a virtue or as a failure. For
n
-ary tensor product
\phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\phi
the corresponding fibre bundle of
\mathcal{H}_{\phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\phi}
spaces, with
\phi
varying over
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
, will be called a
\bigotimes^{n}
-GNS bundle
, and denoted
(\bigotimes^{n}\mathcal{H})\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
. For any
(0,n)
-tensor field
\mathbf{d}f_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathbf{d}f_{n}
on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
with
n\leq\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
there exists a unique representation

\pi_{\phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\phi}\left((\mathbf{d}f_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathbf{d}f_{n})(\phi)\right)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\phi}).

(182)

The same is true for any
n
-form field
, defined as a section of the fibre bundle of an antisymmetric wedge product
\bigwedge^{n}\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, because
\bigwedge^{n}\mathbf{T}_{\phi}^{\maltese}\mathcal{B}_{\star}\subset\bigboxtimes\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\phi}\mathcal{B}_{\star}
. As a result, each smooth section of
\bigwedge^{n}\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
can be represented as a family of bounded operators,

\pi_{\phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\phi}:\bigwedge^{n}\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}_{\phi}\mathcal{B}_{\star}\ni x\mapsto\pi_{\phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\phi}(x)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\phi\otimes\cdots\otimes\phi}),

(183)

acting fibrewise over the
\bigotimes^{n}
-GNS bundle
(\bigotimes^{n}\mathcal{H})\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
. We will call such family a
quantum
n
-form field
over
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
.

The constant function on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
,
\hat{\lambda}:\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}\ni\omega\mapsto\hat{\lambda}(\omega):=\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, is a geometric representation of an algebraic element of a center of
\mathcal{N}
,
\lambda\mathbb{I}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
, in terms of an element of a smooth algebra,
\hat{\lambda}\in{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}};\mathbb{R})
. Such function on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{sa}}
will be called a
global charge
. From this it follows that, provided
\mathcal{B}\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}
, each globally constrained source strength is a global charge.

The set of quantum field one-forms in
\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, considered under its restriction to some quantum model
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
, can be equipped with the additional structure of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
, determined by the structure constants
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{h}}^{abc}
of its adjoint representation by means of

[(\mathbf{d}_{\phi}f)^{a},(\mathbf{d}_{\phi}k)^{b}]_{\mathfrak{h}}=\sum_{c}\epsilon_{\mathfrak{h}}^{abc}(\phi)(\mathbf{d}_{\phi}h)^{c}\;\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}).

(184)

Here
(\mathbf{d}_{\phi}f)^{a}
denotes a Lie algebra representation map
\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow(\mathbf{T}^{\maltese}\mathcal{B}_{\star})
at
\phi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
. Representation of these forms on the fibres of the GNS bundle gives

[\pi_{\phi}((\mathbf{d}_{\phi}f)^{a}),\pi_{\phi}((\mathbf{d}_{\phi}k)^{b})]_{\mathfrak{h}}=\sum_{c}\epsilon_{\mathfrak{h}}^{abc}(\phi)\pi_{\phi}((\mathbf{d}_{\phi}h)^{c})\;\;\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}).

(185)

Note that the Lie algebra structure given by (
185
) is a priori independent of any possible principal
G
-bundle structure of
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
or a principal
G
-bundle
E\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
represented in terms on
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
by means of a local gauge liouvillean. In order to keep the same relationship between source terms
\lambda_{i}(\phi)\pi_{\phi}((\mathbf{d}_{\phi}f_{i})^{a})
in each fibre
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
, one has to set
\lambda_{i}
to be given by a global charge. We will call the source terms
\pi_{\phi}((\mathbf{d}_{\phi}f_{i})^{a})

local gauge quantum fields
, while the corresponding local source models with global source strengths
\lambda_{i}
will be called
local gauge quantum field models
. If an extended liouvillean model
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}),\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a},\lambda_{1},H_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},H_{n})
is equipped with an affine connection
\nabla
such that
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}),\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a},\nabla)
is a gauge geodesic propagation model, while
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}),\lambda_{1},H_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n},H_{n})
is a quantum field model with
H_{i}(\phi)=\pi_{\phi}((\mathbf{d}_{\phi}f_{i})^{a_{i}})

\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, then the
(4+2n)
-tuple

(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N}),\mathbf{A},\mathfrak{a},\nabla,\lambda_{1},(\mathbf{d}f_{1})^{a_{1}},\ldots,\lambda_{n},(\mathbf{d}f_{n})^{a_{n}})

(186)

can be called a ‘quantum field model with gauge geodesic propagation’. If
G
and
\mathfrak{a}
are chosen to agree with the Wigner classification theorem, then such model describes a family of quantum fields together with a quantum particle geodesic propagation. However, while the availability of these constructions is a quite remarkable fact, it is yet unclear how they could be translated to the usual objects of quantum field theory.

3.4
Local liouvillean instruments and correlation functions

The temporal evolution of a local liouvillean model is completely described by the fibrewise evolution

\xi(\omega,t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\omega,t)}\Omega_{\omega},

(187)

which is a generalisation of (
100
) taking local gauge and local source structures on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
into account. The corresponding propagator (transition amplitude) between initial state
\omega
and the final state
\phi
reads

\left\langle{\Omega_{\phi},V_{\phi,\omega}\xi(\omega,t)}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\phi}}=\left\langle{\Omega_{\phi},V_{\phi,\omega}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\omega,t)}\Omega_{\omega}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\phi}}.

(188)

If
\mathcal{L}
is determined only by a given Poisson system
(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B}),h)
with
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}h\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{sa}}

\forall\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
, then the propagator (
188
) reads

\left\langle{\Omega_{\phi},V_{\phi,\omega}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}(h))}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\phi}}=\left\langle{\Omega_{\phi},\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\log(J_{\phi}J_{\phi,\omega})}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\pi_{\omega}(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{F}}_{\omega}(h))}\Omega_{\omega}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\phi}}.

(189)

We will now show that this evolution can be expressed as an instrument acting on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
and parametrised by
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Let us choose some
\psi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
. Then the elements of each fibre of the GNS bundle over
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
can be uniquely mapped to
\mathcal{H}_{\psi}
by means of the standard unitary transition operator
V_{\psi,\omega}=J_{\psi}J_{\psi,\omega}=J_{\psi,\omega}J_{\omega}
, which preserves the positive cones
(see Section
2.2
).
Hence, at each value of
t\in\mathbb{R}
and at each
\psi\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
, the set

\bigcup_{\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})}\{V_{\psi,\omega}\xi(\omega,t)\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\psi}^{+}

(190)

represents completely the evolution in a fibre bundle
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
that is defined by means of a local liouvillean operator. Using the bijective norm continuous homomorphism
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\psi}:\mathcal{H}_{\psi}^{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
(defined as
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\pi}
for
\pi=\pi_{\psi}
), we can represent the mapping

\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\bigcup_{\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})}\left\{V_{\psi,\omega}\xi(\omega,t)\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\psi}^{+}

(191)

as a temporal evolution of subsets of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
,

t\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\psi}\left(\bigcup_{\omega\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})}\left\{V_{\psi,\omega}\xi(\omega,t)\right\}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}.

(192)

The
family of mappings

\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\left\{\mathfrak{I}_{\mathcal{L},\psi}(t):\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\ni\omega\mapsto(\mathfrak{I}_{\mathcal{L},\psi}(t))(\omega):={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\psi}(V_{\psi,\omega}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\omega,t)}\Omega_{\omega})\in\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}\right\}

(193)

will be called a
local liouvillean instrument
(this name may be a bit deceiving, because of nonlocality inherent in the
V_{\phi,\omega}
operation. The uniqueness of standard unitary transition for each pair of elements of
\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
together with bijectivity of
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\psi}
implies

{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\psi}^{\natural}V_{\psi,\omega}\xi={\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\varphi}^{\natural}V_{\varphi,\omega}\xi\;\;\;\forall\xi\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\;\forall\varphi,\omega\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0},

(194)

hence an extended liouvillean instrument does not depend on the choice of
\psi
. In what follows we will denote it by
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathcal{L}(t)}
. Due to bijectivity of
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\natural}_{\psi}
there is an equivalence between the evolution generated on the GNS bundle by the family of extended liouvillean operators and the evolution generated on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{+}
by the extended liouvillean instrument. Thus, one can consider the extended liouvillean operator structure over a given model (including local gauge and local source structures, as well as the isometry
\alpha_{\star}
or a Poisson flow
w^{h}
) as
auxiliary
tools allowing to define suitable extended liouvillean instrument, but otherwise devoid of any foundational meaning.

The GNS bundle allows to construct the
n
-point correlation functions, whenever all quantum states under consideration are faithful. Let
\mathcal{N}
be a W
∗
-algebra, let
\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{N}
. Then we can define the
time independent
n
-point correlation function
as

\left\langle{x_{1}(\phi_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{n}(\phi_{n})}\right\rangle_{\phi_{0}}:=\left\langle{\Omega_{\phi_{0}},V_{\phi_{0},\phi_{1}}\pi_{\phi_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots V_{\phi_{n-1},\phi_{n}}\pi_{\phi_{n}}(x_{n})V_{\phi_{n},\phi_{0}}\Omega_{\phi_{0}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\phi_{0}}}.

(195)

If
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{+}_{\star 0}
is
m
-dimensional,
\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
and
\theta:\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a coordinate system on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
, then (
195
) can be expressed in terms of
\theta
as

\displaystyle\left\langle{x_{1}(\theta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{n}(\theta_{n})}\right\rangle_{\phi(\theta_{0})}=

\displaystyle\left\langle\Omega_{\phi_{0}},V_{\phi(\theta_{0}),\phi(\theta_{1})}\pi_{\phi(\theta_{1})}(x_{1})\cdots\right.

\displaystyle\left.\cdots V_{\phi(\theta_{n-1}),\phi(\theta_{n})}\pi_{\phi(\theta_{n})}(x_{n})V_{\phi(\theta_{n}),\phi(\theta_{0})}\Omega_{\phi(\theta_{0})}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\phi(\theta_{0})}},

(196)

where
\theta_{0},\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\phi_{i}=\phi(\theta_{i}):=\theta^{-1}(\theta_{i})
. Constructions provided in this
paper
allow us to define also the
time dependent
n
-point correlation functions
as

\displaystyle\left\langle{x_{1}(\phi_{1},t_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{n}(\phi_{n},t_{n})}\right\rangle_{\phi_{0}}:=

\displaystyle\left\langle\Omega_{\phi_{0}},V_{\phi_{0},\phi_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{+\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\phi_{1},t_{1})}\pi_{\phi_{1}}(x_{1})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\phi_{1},t_{1})}\cdots\right.

\displaystyle\left.\cdots V_{\phi_{n-1},\phi_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{+\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\phi_{n},t_{n})}\pi_{\phi_{n}}(x_{n})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\phi_{n},t_{n})}V_{\phi_{n},\phi_{0}}\Omega_{\phi_{0}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\phi_{0}}},

(197)

where
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{L}(\phi,t)
is an extended liouvillean. If a reformulation in terms of a coordinate system
\theta
(defined above) is possible, then (
197
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\left\langle{x_{1}(\theta_{1},t_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{n}(\theta_{n},t_{n})}\right\rangle_{\phi(\theta_{0})}=\left\langle\Omega_{\phi(\theta_{0})},V_{\phi(\theta_{0}),\phi(\theta_{1})}\mathrm{e}^{+\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\phi(\theta_{1}),t_{1})}\pi_{\phi(\theta_{1})}(x_{1})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\phi(\theta_{1}),t_{1})}\cdots\right.

\displaystyle\left.\cdots V_{\phi(\theta_{n-1}),\phi(\theta_{n})}\mathrm{e}^{+\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\phi(\theta_{n}),t_{n})}\pi_{\phi(\theta_{n})}(x_{n})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathcal{L}(\phi(\theta_{n}),t_{n})}V_{\phi(\theta_{n}),\phi(\theta_{0})}\Omega_{\phi(\theta_{0})}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\phi(\theta_{0})}}

(198)

Due to different values taken by the components of
\mathcal{L}
operator at different points
(\phi,t)
, the evolution determined by (
187
) and (
197
) does not have to be unitary. It is so only when the dynamics and perturbations in all fibres of the Hilbert bundle are the same.

Equation (
197
) describes how the predictive time dependent content of a quantum model
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
can be determined using the representation of geometric structures on
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{N})
in terms of the algebraic structures on the GNS bundle. However, let us note that this equation is only an example of the variety of possible definitions of the time dependent correlation functions that could be constructed with the help of local liouvilleans and the GNS bundle. Moreover, one could carry the above constructions also for MCP bundle, obtaining different quantitative results. The identification of the proper construction should be based on a more detailed analysis of backwards compatibility with other approaches. In next Section we will approach the derivation of the path integral analogue of the propagator (
188
).

4
Quantum histories

In order to solve the problems of ‘measurement’ and ‘time’ in quantum theory Griffiths
[
125
]
, Omnès
[
257
,
258
,
259
,
260
,
261
,
262
,
263
]
, and Gell-Mann and Hartle
[
108
,
109
,
107
,
134
,
135
,
110
,
136
]
have developed ‘consistent histories’ approach to quantum theory. Isham and Linden
[
159
,
160
,
161
,
162
,
163
]
have proposed a modification of this approach, called the (continuous-time) ‘history projection operator’ approach, which was developed later by Savvidou and Anastopoulos
[
297
,
298
,
12
,
11
,
13
]
.

In Section
4.1
we will recall the elementary mathematical structure of the Isham–Linden approach, in Section
4.2
we will discuss Savvidou’s construction
[
297
,
298
]
of an action operator within this setting, while in Sections
4.3
and
4.4
we will follow the Anastopoulous–Savvidou analysis of the relationship of this framework with the geometric structures on the spaces of pure quantum states, and the Daubechies–Klauder
[
77
,
194
,
365
]
continous-time regularised coherent states phase space path integration. Sections
4.1
-
4.4
do not contain new results. Their aim is to lead us to a refined geometric perspective on the relationship between the Daubechies–Klauder formula and the local liouvilleans. In Section
4.5
we will apply the local liouvillean approach to the Falcone–Takesaki construction of noncommutative flow of weights in order to construct the algebraic analogue of Savvidou’s histories action operator. The discussion of limitations of this construction in the face of the results of Section
3
will lead us to construction of W
∗
-geometric generalisation of the Daubechies–Klauder path integration in Section
4.6
.

4.1
Propositions and evolution

The starting point of the history projection operator approach is consideration of a
history

\varpi
of abstract ‘propositions’
(P_{t_{1}},{P}_{t_{2}},\ldots,{P}_{t_{n}})
about a quantum theoretic model, assigned to an ordered finite sequence
(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n})
, where
t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{n}
, and
t
is interpreted as ‘time’ parameter. Following the ideas of Mittelstaedt
[
242
]
and Stachow
[
332
,
333
]
, Isham
[
159
]
has proposed to specify this entity by the projection operator

P_{\varpi}:=P_{t_{1}}\otimes P_{t_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes P_{t_{n}},

(199)

acting on the Hilbert space

{\cal{V}}_{n}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{H}_{t_{i}}:=\mathcal{H}_{t_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{t_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{t_{n}},

(200)

where
{P}_{t_{i}}
is a projection operator in the
n
-th copy of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{t_{i}}:=\mathcal{H}
of a given quantum model. The history
\varpi
of nonunitary propositions as well as the description of the unitary dynamics are contained in the
class operator
on
{\cal{V}}_{n}
, defined as
[
108
]

C_{\varpi}:=U(t_{0},t_{1}){P}_{t_{1}}U(t_{1},t_{2}){P}_{t_{2}}\cdots U(t_{n-1},t_{n}){P}_{t_{n}}U(t_{n},t_{0}),

(201)

where
U(t_{i},t_{i+1})=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\mathrm{d}tH}
are unitary evolution operators between times
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
, acting on the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{t_{i+1}}
and generated by a self-adjoint hamiltonian operator
H
. For a dynamics generated by the hamiltonian
H
with an initial state described by the density operator
\rho
,
the probability of a history
\varpi
is defined as

p(\varpi;\rho,H):=\mathrm{tr}_{{\cal{V}}_{n}}(C^{*}_{\varpi}\rho C_{\varpi}).

(202)

Using this equation, for two given histories
\varpi
and
\vartheta
, one defines the
histories functional
30
30

30

For historical reasons, this object is usually called ‘decoherence functional’. However, such name suggests that the quantum histories formalism necessary involves the ‘decoherence approach to quantum measurement’ semantics, which is not true. For this reason we choose to change the name of this mathematical object to much more neutral with respect to possible semantics.

[
125
]

\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,H}:\mathfrak{B}({\cal{V}}_{n})\times\mathfrak{B}({\cal{V}}_{n})\ni(P_{\varpi},P_{\vartheta})\mapsto\mathrm{tr}_{{\cal{V}}_{n}}(C^{*}_{\varpi}\rho C_{\vartheta})\in\mathbb{C},

(203)

which, by definition, depends on
\rho
and
H
. It satisfies, for
P_{\varpi}\leq\mathbb{I}-P_{\varkappa}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}(P_{\varpi},P_{\varpi})

\displaystyle\geq 0,

(204)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}(P_{\varpi},P_{\vartheta})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{H}(P_{\vartheta},P_{\varpi})^{*},

(205)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}(0,P_{\varpi})

\displaystyle=0,

(206)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}(\mathbb{I},\mathbb{I})

\displaystyle=1,

(207)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}(P_{\varpi}+P_{\varkappa},P_{\vartheta})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{H}(P_{\varpi},P_{\vartheta})+\mathfrak{H}(P_{\varkappa},P_{\vartheta}).

(208)

Description of a quantum theoretic model provided in terms of the class operator and histories functional is intended to serve as a single replacement for dualistic description of temporal behaviour of model in terms of Schrödinger’s and von Neumann–Lüders’ equations.

The
Born–Jordan–Dirac–Heisenberg
(BJDH)
algebra

[
41
,
40
,
84
]
, generated by the
canonical commutation relations

[\mathrm{q},\mathrm{q}]=0,\;\;\;[\mathrm{p},\mathrm{p}]=0,\;\;\;[\mathrm{q},\mathrm{p}]=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{I},

(209)

is extended in the quantum histories framework to an algebra generated by the relations:

[\mathrm{q}_{t_{i}},\mathrm{q}_{t_{j}}]=0,\;\;\;[\mathrm{p}_{t_{i}},\mathrm{p}_{t_{j}}]=0,\;\;\;[\mathrm{q}_{t_{j}},\mathrm{p}_{t_{k}}]=\mathrm{i}\delta_{jk}\mathbb{I},

(210)

where the operators
\mathrm{q}_{t_{i}}
and
\mathrm{p}_{t_{i}}
are considered as operators defined in the Schrödinger picture for different moments
t_{i}
of time, acting on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{t_{i}}
, while
\mathbb{I}
is a unit element of that algebra. In order to formulate an extension of this formalism to the case of a continuous time
t\in\mathbb{R}
, Isham and Linden
[
161
]
have changed the above relations to the form of the so-called
history algebra
:

\begin{array}[]{c}[\mathrm{q}_{f},\mathrm{q}_{g}]=0,\;\;[\mathrm{p}_{f},\mathrm{p}_{g}]=0,\;\;[\mathrm{q}_{f},\mathrm{p}_{g}]=\mathrm{i}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}tf(t)g(t)\mathbb{I},\end{array}

(211)

where
\mathrm{q}
and
